All other all-triangle results are the regular tetrahedron, octahedron or icosahedron, and each is a cell or vertex figure of its 4D solid. Two supporting propositions fix what the table leaves out.

Proposition 1. No shadow, layer or slice of the 5-, 16-, 24- or 600-cell along the four main directions is a regular deltahedron, except copies of the solid's own cells and vertex figures. Among the others, the only patterns of a convex deltahedron that occur are those of the triangular bipyramid and the octahedron, each with unequal edges.

Proposition 2. A bipyramid over an n-gon with all edges equal exists only for n ≤ 5: for n ≥ 6 the equilateral apex would lie in the plane of the polygon or the solid would cross itself. So the hexagonal and decagonal bipyramid patterns of the 16- and 600-cell have no equilateral form.

4. Variants join differently

TBP-5, the 5-cell's edge-first shadow, has three edges of length √2 and six of length √(7/6). Its faces match no face of J12. Its base edges have a dihedral angle of exactly 120°, so three copies of TBP-5 close face to face round a base edge; regular copies of J12 close no such ring. Its side edges have dihedral angle arccos(1/8), which closes no star of copies, so neither TBP-5 nor J12 fills space.

OCT-16, the 16-cell's edge-first shadow, is a square bipyramid: two copies of the pyramid that joins the centre of a cube to one of its faces, glued base to base. Its dihedral angles are 90° and 120°, and it fills space on its own, face to face: it is the cell of the square bipyramidal honeycomb, a known tiling (§8). The regular octahedron fills space only together with tetrahedra. The angles alone would allow a mixed star round an edge (2·90° + 70.53° + 109.47° = 360°), but the faces of OCT-16 do not match those of the regular octahedron and tetrahedron, so the variant and the regular family do not join face to face.

Both variants therefore differ from their regular counterparts in shape and in how they join, as DICTO expected.

5. The 600-cell's dented patterns, and Kagemusha

The 600-cell's vertex-first shadow is also its central slice, with 42 vertices and 80 triangles. Its equilateral form is an icosahedron with a shallow dent at each vertex (the 12-dented icosahedron). The vertex-first slices at the dodecahedron height (32 vertices, 60 triangles) have two equilateral forms: the excavated dodecahedron, a known deltahedron, with its pyramids dented inward, and the augmented dodecahedron, with its pyramids pointing outward. The names 12-dented icosahedron and augmented dodecahedron describe obvious constructions, and neither solid is claimed new.

Kagemusha. The 600-cell's edge-first slices at the outer heights have 17 vertices and 30 triangles and five-fold symmetry: a tip at each end and three rings of five vertices between them, with the vertices of the middle ring of degree 6. An equilateral form was first found numerically. DICTO's idea of cutting the pattern in halves identified it: cut at the middle ring, and each half is a J11, the gyroelongated pentagonal pyramid (the diminished icosahedron). Kagemusha's equilateral form is two J11 glued on their pentagons. This form is exact by construction, with every edge 1, and all 136 corner-to-corner distances of the numerical form agree with it to 4·10⁻¹⁶. Its five folded edges are the edges of the shared pentagon, where the dihedral angles of the two J11 add to 2 × 100.81° = 201.62°. J11 is the piece with which DICTO's work began: it fills the gaps between dodecahedra and icosahedra.

6. Slice-48 and the six large slices

One of the 600-cell's six larger all-triangle slice patterns, the 48-vertex cell-first slice, has an exact equilateral form with its full symmetry: Slice-48, described at the end of this section. This section gives the method by which all six were solved and the results for each.

The six patterns have 28 to 72 vertices, with rings of high-degree vertices (7 to 10 triangles meeting at a vertex) between tips of degree 5. Cutting them in halves or caps, as for Kagemusha, matched no known piece, so each was solved directly. The exact symmetry group of each slice equals the full symmetry group of its triangle pattern. Keeping that symmetry, one vertex of each orbit is placed in the subspace fixed by its stabiliser, and each orbit of edges gives one equation |edge|² = 1; every system is square (as many equations as unknowns). The number of complex solutions of each system was found exactly over ℚ(√5) (Singular); every solution was found and refined to 120 digits, and a system counts as complete only when the number of solutions found equals the exact count. Every real solution was then tested for a proper embedding.

Slice (600-cell)Vertices, trianglesSymmetrySolutions (complex / real)Equilateral form
vertex-first, at ±(√5−1)72, 140Ih8 / 4none: vertices coincide
edge-first, at ±(3+√5)/232, 60D5d128 / 48none: edges pierce faces, or faces touch
face-first, at ±(4+2√5)/330, 56D3d464 / 104none: edges pierce faces, or faces touch
cell-first, at ±(5+3√5)/428, 52Td32 / 12none: edges pierce or cross, vertices coincide
cell-first, at ±(3+√5)/242, 80Td96 / 32none: every solution crosses itself
cell-first, at ±(2+√5)/248, 92Td192 / 48Slice-48 (one shape; its two solutions differ by a half-turn)

The systems of the 72-, 32-, 28- and 42-vertex slices were also solved with only their rotation group kept, and again no form exists; the real solution counts of the same four systems were rechecked exactly.

Slice-48. The 48-vertex cell-first slice of the 600-cell has exactly one equilateral form with its tetrahedral symmetry Td: 48 vertices (12 of degree 8, 36 of degree 5), 138 equal edges and 92 triangles. It is not convex: 24 of its vertices lie on its hull, and its dihedral angles are 81.75°, 130.98°, 135.83°, 143.83°, 191.03°, 197.94°, 216.17° and 243.20°. At edge length 1, its nearest non-adjacent parts are 0.069 apart; the embedding was confirmed with exact orientation tests on 85-digit rational coordinates.

Unlike Kagemusha, Slice-48 contains no piece of the icosahedron: it has no dihedral angle of 138.19° and no pentagonal cap. It is not among the non-convex deltahedra we checked (§8); since we did not read all the original papers, we do not claim it new. Forms with less symmetry than the slice's group are not excluded; the rotation-only systems of the 30- and 48-vertex slices did not finish in 50 minutes.

7. A note on the 600-cell's sections

The vertex-first "sections" of the 600-cell that are often listed — icosahedron, dodecahedron, icosahedron, icosidodecahedron — are the outlines of its vertex layers. A true cut at the dodecahedron height or at the equator also meets edges that cross it, and adds their midpoints: the true central section is the 42-vertex solid of §5, with the same pattern, edge lengths and angles as the shadow. This was checked exactly and with an independent floating-point computation.

8. Prior art and credit

Coxeter's Regular Polytopes treats the polytopes, their projections and the vertex-first sections of the 600-cell. The eight convex deltahedra are classical (Freudenthal and van der Waerden 1947); non-convex equilateral realisations and dented variants of deltahedral patterns are studied by Tsuruta et al. (2014). For Slice-48 we also checked the biform deltahedra (Cundy; Olshevsky), the isohedral deltahedra (Shephard; McNeill) and Trigg's spiral deltahedra, through their Wikipedia summaries. The square bipyramidal honeycomb and the excavated dodecahedron are known. In the sources checked, we did not find the comparison of the 4D shadows and slices with the deltahedra, the attachability of the variants, or the identification of Kagemusha.

9. Conclusion

Main result. The triangle-faced shadows and slices of the four-dimensional solids give deltahedra of two new kinds.
(a) Variants that join differently. The 5-cell's edge-first shadow (TBP-5) has the pattern of J12, and three copies of it close round an edge. The 16-cell's edge-first shadow (OCT-16) has the pattern of the octahedron, and it fills space on its own.
(b) Equal-edged solids from the 600-cell. Four of its shadows and slices can be rebuilt exactly with every edge the same length, keeping their pattern of triangles; all four forms are non-convex.
600-cell shadow or sliceTrianglesRebuilt with equal edges
vertex-first shadow = central slice (42 vertices)8012-dented icosahedron: an icosahedron with a shallow dent at each of its 12 vertices
vertex-first slices at the dodecahedron height (32 vertices)60excavated dodecahedron (dents inward; known) and augmented dodecahedron (pyramids outward)
edge-first slices at the outer heights (17 vertices)30Kagemusha: two J11 glued on their pentagons
cell-first slices with 48 vertices92Slice-48: tetrahedral symmetry, dihedral angles 81.75° to 243.20°

Kagemusha is built from two copies of J11, the piece with which DICTO's work began. Slice-48 is the largest of the four: 92 equal triangles with the full tetrahedral symmetry of its slice, found by solving all six large slices of the 600-cell exactly.

10. The walls each shadow keeps

This section is Section 10 of paper #110, Walls of Silence, Version 2 (DICTO DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24272477, all versions), given here in full; the walls are defined in Chapter 10, and the rest of #110 is in Appendix B.

A shadow flattens almost everything. A few flat slices through the centre survive it whole, and every one of them is a wall of the Queens.

The White Queen has two kinds of wall, all of them flat slices through her centre: her 60 mirrors, and 300 cross walls (DICTO's name), each parallel to a pair of her opposite cells. The 360 walls are the Red Queen's walls too (Chapter 10). When a Queen is cast into our space along a direction d, a wall with normal n stays flat in the shadow, as a plane through the shadow's centre, exactly when n is perpendicular to d. The normals of the cross walls point to the White Queen's cell centres and those of the mirrors to the Red Queen's, and a cell is seen side-on exactly when its centre direction is perpendicular to d; so in every shadow the walls kept are the central planes parallel to the cells seen side-on.

In the four main views the kept walls number (mirrors, cross walls) = (15, 30), (6, 10), (4, 12) and (6, 27): corner-, edge-, face- and cell-first for the White Queen, which are cell-, pentagon-, edge- and corner-first for the Red Queen. A solid in the shadow whose edges all lie, with the centre, in kept walls is a possible Vacuus (DICTO's term; the Vacuus is defined in Chapter 10). In the White Queen's corner-first shadow the icosahedra and the dodecahedron are possible Vacuus, 30 of 30 plates on mirrors; in the Red Queen's cell-first shadow, the dodecahedra and a truncated icosahedron at height ±(1/8 + φ/4), with 30 mirror and 60 cross plates (Figures 5.4 and 5.5).

DICTO observed that the shadows "seem to carry interconnected lattices of possible vacuus". They do, exactly: the shadows of each of the four types reach all 360 walls between them, and joining two shadows when they keep a wall in common gives one connected graph. Every shadow is a window on one wall system; Chapter 10 gives the law of its walls, and Chapter 11 the shapes that live in them.

* Mosseri–Sadoc tile t6, scaled by 1/φ (Mosseri and Sadoc, 1982). Rediscovered by DICTO as AXE. ** Mosseri–Sadoc tile t4, scaled by 1/φ (Mosseri and Sadoc, 1982). Rediscovered by DICTO as CLEO. *** Mosseri–Sadoc tile t3 (Mosseri and Sadoc, 1982). DICTO's name: Tricap. **** The icosahedron face pyramid (classical; Mackay, 1962). Rediscovered by DICTO as the Cleo-Icos.

Part III. The Queens

Chapter 6. The White Queen 600-cell

From paper #63, DICTO DOI 10.5281/zenodo.23690856

An exact atlas of the slices and shadows of the 600-cell

Abstract. The 600-cell is the four-dimensional solid built from 600 regular tetrahedra, twenty round every vertex. The aim is a definitive, proved picture of it. We give a complete exact atlas of its three-dimensional slices in its four main directions (through a vertex, the middle of an edge, the centre of a triangle and the centre of a cell). Every slice is computed exactly, in numbers of the form a + b√5 with a and b rational. Between two neighbouring vertex heights a slice keeps one pattern of faces, so the cuts at the vertex heights and one cut inside each gap give every slice pattern there is: 62 patterns on one side of the centre (9, 17, 21 and 15 in the four directions). Exactly twelve are made only of triangles, and we identify each one. The largest slices have 144 vertices. With the four shadows of the 600-cell, this completes the atlas. Each shadow resolves exactly into two halves, its front cells and its back cells, each filling it once; along the six turnings between the main views, the set of cells in front changes at only 14 moments. Round one vertex the 600 cells form 15 shells, each of one kind of piece, the innermost a core of 20 Cleo-Icos*. The slices as they are, convex and exact, with faces of many sizes, are the subject of this chapter. A short side note records what happens when a slice is forced to have equal edges: of six large all-triangle slices only one can be. That slice, a convex egg of 92 triangles, is the cell-first section at height 1 + √5/2** (Boole Stott, 1900); its forced equal-edged form, Fallen Humpty, has lost the egg's rounded shape.

1. Begin at the beginning

“Begin at the beginning,” the King said gravely, “and go on till you come to the end: then stop.” — Alice's Adventures in Wonderland, ch. 12

That is the plan of this chapter: start at the centre of the 600-cell and cut it, height by height, until the cut leaves it at the tip. The 600-cell can be assembled into a definitive picture, every part proved. Our previous paper (#62, Shadows of the Fourth Dimension) found which of its slices have only triangular faces, and which of those can be rebuilt with every edge equal; here we map every slice. The problem is old. Aristotle wrote that among solids the pyramid fills space (On the Heavens III.8); his commentators argued over how many tetrahedra meet round a point, twelve or twenty, and Thomas Bradwardine saw that the icosahedron cuts from its centre into twenty equal tetrahedra without deciding whether they are regular (Lagarias and Zong, 2012; our #44, Aristotle's Pyramid). They are not: they are the Cleo-Icos. Kepler nested the Platonic solids in shells (Mysterium Cosmographicum, 1596). In the 600-cell, found by Schläfli about 1852, twenty regular tetrahedra do close round every vertex, and §6 shows its 600 cells in shells round such a core. The title is DICTO's: the 600-cell belongs to the White Queen of Through the Looking-Glass, who practised believing impossible things (§7).

2. Method

The 600-cell is taken with its 120 vertices at distance 2 from the centre: the 8 permutations of (±2, 0, 0, 0), the 16 points (±1, ±1, ±1, ±1) and the 96 even permutations of (±φ, ±1, ±1/φ, 0), where φ = (1+√5)/2. Its edges have length √5 − 1. All arithmetic is exact in ℚ(√5). For each direction d, the height of a point x is x·d, and we sort the vertices by height. The slice at height h is the convex hull of the vertices at that height together with the points where edges of the 600-cell cross that height. Hulls are proposed in floating point, and every face is then verified exactly. The checks, run on every slice, are these. Repeated points are merged by exact comparison. For each proposed face, the plane through three of its corners is checked to support the slice: every point lies on it or strictly on one side, decided by the exact sign of a number in ℚ(√5). The face is then all the points on that plane, ordered by an exact two-dimensional hull, and turned to face outward by an exact sign. Finally every edge of the slice must lie in exactly two faces, and the counts must satisfy V − E + F = 2. A closed surface of supporting faces is the boundary of the hull, so a face missed in floating point would show as an edge in only one face.

Lemma (one pattern per gap). Let h₁ < h₂ be two neighbouring vertex heights. At every height strictly between them the slice has the same pattern of faces. Proof. An edge of the 600-cell crosses the cut at height h exactly when its two ends lie on opposite sides of h. No vertex has a height strictly between h₁ and h₂, so as h moves through the gap no vertex changes side, and the same edges cross the cut. Inside the gap each face of the slice is the cross-section of one tetrahedral cell, with its corners at the crossing points on that cell's edges, and two faces share an edge of the slice exactly when their cells share a triangle that is cut. Which cells and triangles are cut, and through which edges, is therefore the same throughout the gap, and so is the pattern of faces. Hence the cuts at the vertex heights and one cut inside each gap give every slice pattern. The lemma is about the pattern only: edge lengths and angles change with the height.

In each direction, d is the vertex, the edge midpoint, the triangle centre or the cell centre itself, not a unit vector: vertex-first d = (2, 0, 0, 0); edge-first d = ((5+√5)/4, 1/2, (√5−1)/4, 0), the midpoint of the edge from (2, 0, 0, 0) to (φ, 1, 1/φ, 0); face-first d = (1+√5/3, 2/3, 0, 0), the centre of the triangle with corners (2, 0, 0, 0), (φ, 1, 1/φ, 0) and (φ, 1, −1/φ, 0); cell-first d = ((7+3√5)/8, (3+√5)/8, 0, 1/4), the centre of the cell with corners (2, 0, 0, 0), (φ, 1, 1/φ, 0), (φ, 1, −1/φ, 0) and (φ, 1/φ, 0, 1). Each d is the average of the corners named. Since d is not a unit vector, heights compare within one direction only. The 600-cell is centrally symmetric, so the far half of each direction mirrors the near half; we list the near half, from the centre to the tip.

3. The atlas

DirectionVertex heights (centre to tip)GapsSlice patternsTipLargest slice
vertex-first549a vertex132 vertices
edge-first9817an edge142 vertices
face-first111021a triangle144 vertices
cell-first8715a tetrahedron144 vertices
total62

Every slice has only triangles and four-sided faces. Each face is the cross-section of one tetrahedral cell, a triangle or a four-sided face, or else a triangle of the 600-cell lying in the cut. A four-sided face appears exactly where the cut crosses a cell through four of its edges. The atlas, which cuts each gap at its middle height, has 1636 four-sided faces, and exactly 46 of them are squares, checked exactly. The squares are exactly the cuts through the middle of a cell, parallel to two opposite edges: each is the equator of the regular octahedron that joins the midpoints of that cell's six edges (edge (√5 − 1)/2 = 1/φ). Moved off the middle of the cell, such a cut is a rectangle. The 104 kites are explained in the same way: a kite appears exactly where a cell sits symmetrically across the cut, turned so that its four corners lie at four different heights, −p, −q, q and p from the cut. The half-turn of the cell that exchanges the corners at −p and p, and those at −q and q, reverses the heights and so maps the cut onto itself; on the four-sided face it fixes two opposite corners and exchanges the other two, a mirror along a diagonal (checked exactly: 104 kites, 104 such cells). The remaining faces are rectangles, isosceles trapezia and general quadrilaterals.

4. The twelve all-triangle slices

Theorem. Exactly twelve of the 62 slice patterns are made only of triangles: eleven at vertex heights and one inside a gap.

The other 50 slice patterns all contain four-sided faces. Of the twelve, the two regular icosahedra and the tetrahedron are the 600-cell's own vertex figure and cell; the other nine are the all-triangle patterns studied in #62.

5. The four shadows

The shadows are the projections of the 600-cell along the four directions, and all four are convex. The vertex-first shadow has 42 vertices and 80 triangles, the same pattern as the central slice (#62). The edge-first shadow has 52 vertices, 60 triangles and 20 four-sided faces; the face-first shadow 56 vertices, 84 triangles and 12 four-sided faces; the cell-first shadow 44 vertices, 72 triangles and 6 four-sided faces.

6. The shadows resolved, and the Cleo shells

Halves. In each main view, every cell of the 600-cell faces the viewer, faces away, or is seen side-on, decided exactly by the sign of (cell centre)·d. The front cells fill the shadow exactly once, without gaps or overlaps, and so do the back cells. Checked exactly: in each view the projected volumes of the front cells add up to the volume of the shadow, and so do those of the back cells. The side-on cells flatten into the shadow's surface. The front cells together are the shadow's front layer. Two front cells are of one kind when their projections have the same six edge lengths.

ViewFrontBackSide-onKinds of front piece
vertex-first270270607
edge-first2902902022
face-first2882882433
cell-first2732735420

The six turnings. Any two of the four main views span a plane in which the view can turn from one to the other: six turnings in all. Along a turning the front layer changes only at a moment when a cell turns side-on. At such a moment cells change sides in opposite pairs, a cell coming forward as the cell opposite it goes back, so the number in front stays the same. Between two such moments the front layer keeps one pattern (the shadow's counterpart of the lemma of §2). Computed exactly in ℚ(√5), the six turnings (vertex–edge 18°, vertex–triangle 20.905°, vertex–cell 22.239°, edge–triangle 10.812°, edge–cell 13.283°, triangle–cell 7.761°) contain 14 such moments and 20 patterns between them; with the four main views, this accounts for every view along the turnings. Away from the moments the front layer holds 300 cells, or 298 or 294 where 4 or 12 cells stay side-on along the whole turning. One moment on the vertex–cell turning lies 15.52° from the vertex-first view, at the angle whose cosine is (1 + 3√5)/8: that is 180° minus the 600-cell's dihedral angle, 164.48°.

The Cleo shells. Grouped by the height of their centres along one vertex, the 600 cells form 15 shells of 20, 20, 30, 60, 60, 60, 20, 60, 20, 60, 60, 60, 30, 20 and 20 cells. DICTO noticed that this list reads the same backwards. The angles from the vertex to the shells' cell centres mirror it too: 22.24°, 37.76°, 45°, 55.11°, 69.30°, 77.38°, 82.24°, 90°, then 97.76° and on to 157.76°, and each shell's angle and its mirror shell's angle add up to exactly 180°. Both follow from the 600-cell's symmetry through its centre, which carries shell k to shell 14 − k and the vertex to the opposite vertex. Seen in perspective from just beyond the opposite vertex (eye at height −21/10 when the vertex is at 2), the cells of each shell are all of one kind: they have the same six edge lengths, checked exactly. The core is the innermost shell, the 20 cells round the vertex. In this view their common corner is the centre, and their twelve other corners, the vertex's twelve neighbours, form a regular icosahedron about it (§4); so each core cell is the central pyramid of a regular icosahedron, the piece DICTO named the Cleo-Icos (#19). Mosseri and Sadoc flatten the 600-cell into ordinary space to describe icosahedral order in materials (§8); the icosahedron of 20 Cleo-Icos is the 600-cell's vertex cluster brought flat. In DICTO's words, the Cleo-Icos is the 600-cell's original tetrahedron as it must look in flat space: twenty of them fill the regular icosahedron exactly. The shells also explain the vertex-first shadow: its front layer is exactly the core and the next six shells (270 cells), its side-on cells are the middle shell (60), and its back layer is the other seven shells.

DICTO's radial cell projection. In the Polyhedraverse app, DICTO's radial cell projection (RCP) builds the 600-cell cell by cell, each new cell folded across a face of one already placed. It is his rediscovery of the classical reflection construction (Coxeter). The fold between neighbouring cells is the angle with cosine (1 + 3√5)/8, 15.52°: the same angle as the moment on the vertex–cell turning above. Counted in steps from one cell to a face-to-face neighbour, the 600 cells fall into 16 shells of 1, 4, 12, 24, 36, 52, 68, 76, 78, 72, 64, 56, 40, 12, 4 and 1 cells, checked here on the exact model (schlegel_shells.py). The 600-cell thus unfolds in three ways from one exact model: in DICTO's 16 steps from a cell, in 31 layers of height from a cell, and in the 15 Cleo shells round a vertex.

7. A side note: six impossible things, and Humpty Dumpty's fall

“Why, sometimes I've believed as many as six impossible things before breakfast.” — the White Queen, Through the Looking-Glass, ch. 5

Six of the all-triangle slices are large, with 28 to 72 vertices, and match none of the eight convex deltahedra: the slices with 72 (vertex-first), 32 (edge-first), 30 (face-first), and 48, 42 and 28 (cell-first) vertices. #62 tested each for whether its triangles can all be made equal while its full symmetry is kept. For five of them this is impossible. For the 48-vertex slice it is possible in exactly one way, and the result has a name.

“All the King's horses and all the King's men / Couldn't put Humpty Dumpty in his place again.” — Through the Looking-Glass, ch. 6

The real 48-vertex slice, at height 1 + √5/2 in the cell-first direction, is Boole Stott's section, the slice of Humpty Dumpty's fall: a convex, egg-like solid of 92 triangles, with edge lengths in ratios up to (5+√5)/2 ≈ 3.62 (Figure 6.2). Forced to have all 138 edges equal, it becomes Fallen Humpty (Slice-48 in #62): a non-convex solid that folds in and out, with dihedral angles from 81.75° to 243.20°, where the egg was smooth. As DICTO remarked, the rhyme describes the situation exactly: the equal triangles put him together again, but not in his place. What the 600-cell shows is the egg; Fallen Humpty is what our demand for equal triangles makes of it.

“When I use a word,” Humpty Dumpty said in rather a scornful tone, “it means just what I choose it to mean—neither more nor less.” — ch. 6

8. Prior art and credit

Alicia Boole Stott (1900; encouraged by P. H. Schoute) studied series of three-dimensional sections of the regular four-dimensional polytopes and made complete sets of models of the sections of the 600-cell and the 120-cell; two sets of 600-cell section models are kept at the University of Groningen, and a further set is at the Faulkes Institute, Cambridge. Coxeter's Regular Polytopes treats the polytopes and the vertex-first sections. We have now read her paper, "On certain series of sections of the regular four-dimensional hypersolids" (Verhandelingen der Koninklijke Akademie van Wetenschappen te Amsterdam, Eerste Sectie, 7, no. 3, 1900, with 5 plates). Her series for the 600-cell is cell-first: she cuts by spaces parallel to one bounding tetrahedron, starting with that tetrahedron and passing through the layers of vertices to the middle, after which "the other sections are now repeated in reverse order"; she finds each section by construction, draws it as a flat plan with its faces coloured by kind of tetrahedron (her diagrams I–VII), builds it as a model, and counts the 600 tetrahedra by colour across the series (her §17). This matches our eight cell-first cuts through layers of corners, from the tip to the centre. Her series has no cuts inside the gaps between layers and no vertex-, edge- or face-first series of the 600-cell, and she does not treat shadows. Her work is the precedent for this atlas, and its cell-first sections at the corner layers are hers. Mosseri and Sadoc flatten the 600-cell into ordinary space to describe icosahedral order in materials (C. R. Physique 15, 2014, doi:10.1016/j.crhy.2013.09.006); that its cells then become Cleo-Icos was noted in the Cleo-Icos reading study of 14 October 2026, made on DICTO's idea. Counts of cells by layer round a vertex of the 600-cell are a natural tabulation and may be known; we did not find them in the sources checked. We did not find in the sources checked: an exact atlas in all four directions including the gaps between vertex heights (Boole Stott's series is the cell-first one through the corner layers), the list of all-triangle slices, or the exact solid of the 48-corner section.

9. Conclusion

The 600-cell has 62 slice patterns between its centre and its tips in its four main directions (the far half mirrors the near half), all computed exactly here; exactly twelve are made only of triangles, and each is identified. With the four shadows this is a complete, proved atlas of the White Queen's solid, every slice shown as it really is. Its shadows resolve into exact halves, and its 600 cells stand in 15 shells round one vertex, each shell of one kind of piece, the innermost a core of 20 Cleo-Icos. Its most graceful slice is the 48-vertex egg, a section of Boole Stott's series. Forcing equal edges, the aim of #62, succeeds for that one large slice only, and its result, Fallen Humpty, is less graceful than the egg it came from.

Chapter 7. Between the Queens

From paper #76, DICTO DOI 10.5281/zenodo.23780068

The five stages from the 600-cell to the 120-cell, built from the White Queen, with their slices and shadows

Abstract. In #63 the White Queen is the 600-cell, and in #65 the Red Queen, the 120-cell, is her dual. The four-dimensional icosahedron with hexagons, and the stages that lead from one queen to the other, are DICTO's ideas. Cut the White Queen's corners, deeper and deeper, and she passes through five uniform four-dimensional solids before she becomes the Red Queen: the truncated and rectified 600-cell, the bitruncated 600-cell (the midpoint, whose cells are 120 truncated icosahedra and 600 truncated tetrahedra), and the rectified and truncated 120-cell. All seven are classical: Alicia Boole Stott was the first to list the semiregular four-dimensional solids, in 1910, and they are Wythoff's and Coxeter's uniform polytopes. We build each stage from the White Queen's own corners, edges, triangles and cells, prove the cut positions that make every edge equal (1/3, 1/2, 3/5, the triangle centre and (5 − √5)/10), and give the complete atlas of their slices and shadows in the queens' four directions: 1402 slices from the centre to the tips, and 20 shadows. Main theorem: the numbers of slices and the shadows of Table 2, with both ends equal to the exact atlases of #63 and #65; and in every direction the shadows of the truncated and the rectified 600-cell have the same number of faces, and so do those of the rectified and the truncated 120-cell. The stages are in the app: a slider in the White Queen world runs from White to Red.

1. From White to Red

The White Queen and the Red Queen are polar partners. Between them lies a path of solids, each made by cutting her corners a little deeper.

In ordinary space the icosahedron becomes the dodecahedron in the same way. Cut its twelve corners a third of the way along each edge and it becomes the truncated icosahedron: 12 pentagons and 20 hexagons. Cut to the middle of each edge and it is the icosidodecahedron; cut further and it becomes the truncated dodecahedron, and at last the dodecahedron. Take the four-dimensional version: an icosahedron with hexagons, in the fourth dimension. It is the midpoint of the same path one dimension up, from the White Queen to the Red Queen.

2. The five stages, built from the White Queen

Each stage is a set of points chosen on the White Queen's own edges, triangles or cells; nothing else is needed.

We take the White Queen of #63: 120 corners, 720 edges of length e, 1200 triangles, 600 tetrahedra. Her polar partner's corners are the centres of her cells, and two of them are joined when the two cells share a triangle (#65): 1200 Red Queen edges of length f.

bitruncated 600-cellon each triangle, 3/5 of the way from the middle of each of its edges to its centre36007200120 truncated icosahedra, 600 truncated tetrahedra
rectified 120-cellthe centre of each triangle12003600120 icosidodecahedra, 600 tetrahedra
truncated 120-cellon each Red Queen edge, (5 − √5)/10 of the way from each end24004800120 truncated dodecahedra, 600 tetrahedra
Red Queen, 120-cellthe centres of her cells6001200120 dodecahedra

Table 1. The seven solids. The counts are the classical ones; we checked them on our models, together with the cells (each cell is the set of corners highest along one of the White Queen's 120 corner directions or 600 cell directions).

Proposition 1 (the cuts). With the corners of Table 1, every edge of each stage has one length exactly when the cuts are at 1/3 (truncated 600-cell), 3/5 (bitruncated) and (5 − √5)/10 (truncated 120-cell); the rectified stages need no choice.

Proof. Truncated 600-cell. Its edges are of two kinds: round each White Queen corner, between the points on two of her edges at that corner, length s·e (two edges at a corner meet at 60°); and along each of her edges, between its two points, length (1 − 2s)·e. They are equal exactly when s = 1/3. Bitruncated. In a triangle abc let mab be the middle of ab and g the centre; the corner on ab is q = (1 − l)·mab + l·g. Within the triangle, qab to qac has length (1 − l)·|mab − mac| = (1 − l)·e/2. Between the triangles abc and abd of one tetrahedron, the two corners on ab are l·|gabc − gabd| = l·|c − d|/3 = l·e/3 apart. Equal exactly when (1 − l)/2 = l/3, that is l = 3/5. Truncated 120-cell. Along a Red Queen edge the two corners are (1 − 2t)·f apart; round a Red Queen corner P, the corners on the edges PQ and PR are t·|Q − R| apart, and Q and R, two neighbours of P on one pentagon, are a pentagon diagonal φ·f apart. Equal exactly when t·φ = 1 − 2t, that is t = 1/(φ + 2) = (5 − √5)/10. Rectified stages. Middles of edges, and centres of triangles, are joined only to their nearest neighbours, all at one distance (e/2 and e/3). ∎

The cuts were also found numerically, independently, by asking that every corner's nearest neighbours be equally far; the values agree to 10−14 (study cuts.py). In the app the stages are built the same way, from the White Queen's model, and checked: corners and edges as in Table 1, all corners on one sphere, all edges of one length.

3. Main theorem: the atlas

A four-dimensional solid can be seen by its shadows and by its slices. We give both, for every stage, in the four directions the queens were mapped in.

The four main directions are those of #63: along a White Queen corner (vertex first), the middle of an edge (edge first), the centre of a triangle (face first) and the centre of a cell (cell first). In each, the slices are cut at every height where a corner lies and halfway between neighbouring heights, from the centre to the tip; between two neighbouring heights the slice keeps one pattern (#63, Lemma), so this list is complete. All seven solids have a centre of symmetry, so the other half repeats this one.

Theorem. (1) In the four main directions the five stages have the numbers of slices, the numbers of different slice patterns and the shadows of Table 2: 288, 138, 478, 180 and 318 slices from the centre to the tips, 1402 in all. (2) At the two ends the same count gives the exact atlases: the White Queen's 62 slices and 4 shadows (#63) and the Red Queen's 124 slices and 4 shadows (#65). (3) In every direction the shadows of the truncated and the rectified 600-cell have the same number of faces (110, 232, 272, 194), and so do the shadows of the rectified and the truncated 120-cell (102, 252, 296, 242).

SolidSlices v · e · f · cAllPatterns v · e · f · cShadows, corners/faces: v · e · f · c
White Queen9 · 17 · 21 · 15627 · 15 · 19 · 1442/80 · 52/80 · 56/96 · 44/78
truncated 600-cell29 · 85 · 103 · 7128821 · 77 · 98 · 62192/110 · 280/232 · 312/272 · 240/194
rectified 600-cell17 · 39 · 49 · 3313816 · 35 · 49 · 33132/110 · 150/232 · 168/272 · 144/194

Red Queen15 · 33 · 45 · 311248 · 29 · 40 · 2480/42 · 160/92 · 180/104 · 158/84

Table 2. The atlas. v, e, f, c: the White Queen's vertex-first, edge-first, face-first and cell-first directions (for the Red Queen these are her cell, pentagon, edge and corner directions, in that order). A pattern is a slice's corners, edges, faces and faces by sides; two slices of one direction with the same counts are one pattern. Every three-dimensional slice and every shadow satisfies Euler's formula V − E + F = 2.

Proof and checks. Each slice is the convex hull of the solid's corners at that height and the points where its edges cross it, found in the three-dimensional space of the cut; faces are the coplanar parts of the hull, each counted by its true corners. The computation is in double precision with a tolerance of 10−9; it is checked at both ends against the exact atlases in ℚ(√5) of #63 and #65 (the numbers of slices in all four directions, 62 and 124, and the corners and faces of all 8 shadows agree), and by Euler's formula on every three-dimensional slice and every shadow. The shadows were computed a second time by the app's own hull code: the two agree on all 23 shadows the study gives; in the cell-first direction the study's merging of coplanar faces failed Euler's check for the five stages, and those five shadows are the app's, each checked by Euler's formula. Part (3) is read off the table. ∎

All triangles. The White Queen has twelve slices made only of triangles (#63; here 5, 2, 1 and 4 in the four directions). Of the stages, only the truncated 600-cell (3, vertex first), the rectified 600-cell (1 vertex first, 1 cell first), the rectified 120-cell (1, cell first) and the truncated 120-cell (3, cell first) have any; the bitruncated midpoint has none. The last slice at the tip is a single corner, an edge, a polygon or a whole cell, depending on the solid and the direction (study data).

4. The midpoint

Halfway from White to Red the solid is its own partner: cut from either queen, the same shape appears.

The bitruncated 600-cell is also the bitruncated 120-cell: cutting the Red Queen's corners as deeply from her side reaches the same solid, which is why it stands at the middle of the path. Its 120 truncated icosahedra are the icosahedra with hexagons of Section 1; its 600 truncated tetrahedra fill the places of the White Queen's cells. Its shadows are the richest in Table 2, up to 636 corners; its slices number 478, a third of all the slices of the five stages.

See also paper #104, Scutoid Tissues in Four Dimensions (DICTO DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24111864), Theorem 4, the balance law (proved): when the cells of a simple 4-polytope are of two kinds, two of each at every corner, the swaps of a generic sweep that exchange a contact of one kind for a contact of the other are equally many in the two directions; for this midpoint, with the 600 truncated tetrahedra as one kind and the 120 truncated icosahedra as the other, 601 each way and 964 mixed. The law is stated for that condition only. Chapter 10 retells the Mirror Law of #107, by which the hexagons where the two kinds meet lie on no mirror.

5. What is new and what is known

  • Known, credited: the seven solids, their counts and their cells (Boole Stott 1910, who first listed all the semiregular four-dimensional solids; Wythoff's construction; Coxeter's uniform polytopes); the 600-cell's and 120-cell's slices along the cell direction (Boole Stott 1900); the exact atlases of the two queens (#63, #65).
  • New here, as far as the sources checked show: the five stages built from the White Queen's own elements with these cut positions and their short proofs (Proposition 1); the complete slice and shadow atlas of the five stages in the queens' four directions (Theorem 1, Table 2); the equal face counts of the paired shadows (Theorem 3); the stage slider in the app. We found no published atlas of the slices of these five solids; programs that draw slices of any uniform polytope exist, and their output is not a tabulated, checked atlas.
  • Next: an exact version of the atlas in ℚ(√5), as #63 and #65; a proof of part (3) from the solids' symmetry.

6. In the app

Kaleidohedra and Rhombiverse, the White Queen world, Shadow view: the slider "From White to Red" runs from the White Queen through the five stages to the Red Queen, in all four directions and the turnings between them; the caption gives each stage's corners and cells and its shadow's corners and faces. The link https://kaleidohedra.dictospheres.com/?open=whitequeen:t12 opens the midpoint.

DICTO's idea: the four-dimensional icosahedron with hexagons, and the stages between the queens; the names White Queen and Red Queen are his. Licence CC BY-SA 4.0.

Chapter 8. The Red Queen 120-cell

From paper #65, DICTO DOI 10.5281/zenodo.23716140

Aristotle's five pyramids as the cores of the regular four-dimensional solids

Abstract. Aristotle wrote that among solids the pyramid fills space, and the pyramids cut from the centres of the five Platonic solids have been argued over ever since. We prove that all five of these pyramids are the cores of the regular four-dimensional solids, seen in perspective: the tetrahedron, octahedron and icosahedron pyramids are the shadows of the cells round one corner of the 5-cell, the 16-cell and the 600-cell, and the cube, octahedron and dodecahedron pyramids are the shadows of the sides of the four-dimensional pyramid over one cell of the tesseract, the 24-cell and the 120-cell. Each shadow has exactly the proportions of Aristotle's pyramid, and the five come in polar pairs. The 120-cell, polar of the White Queen 600-cell of #63 and named the Red Queen by DICTO, is the White Queen's dual: its 600 corners are her 600 cell centres, its edges join cells that share a triangle, its 720 pentagons ring her 720 edges and its 120 dodecahedra ring her 120 corners; its corners stand in her fifteen Cleo shells, its 120 dodecahedra in nine layers round one, and its core is the dodecahedron cut into twelve pentagonal pyramids where hers is the icosahedron cut into twenty Cleo-Icos*. No convex regular solid has dodecahedra round a corner, but the star polytope {5/2,5,3} does: its twelve golden spikes at a corner cast exactly Aristotle's dodecahedron pyramids. Her full atlas is exact: 124 slice patterns in the four main directions, passing cell-first through the Archimedean solids of the icosahedral family, and four shadows, each filled exactly once by its front cells and once by its back cells, each the polar of the White Queen's middle slice.

1. From the White Queen to the Red Queen

In #63, the White Queen 600-cell, 600 regular tetrahedra stand round 120 corners, and round one corner they fall into fifteen shells whose core is twenty Cleo-Icos filling a regular icosahedron (#19, #44). Take her polar swap: every corner of one solid becomes a cell of the other. The polar of the 600-cell is the 120-cell, 120 regular dodecahedra round 600 corners. DICTO named it the Red Queen, after the other queen of Through the Looking-Glass. The dodecahedral pyramid, the one of Aristotle's five pyramids the White Queen lacks, should sit at the Red Queen's core, and all five pyramids should be four-dimensional shadows. They are.

2. The Red Queen, the White Queen's dual

We build the 120-cell from the exact model of #63, in ℚ(√5). Its corners are the centres of the White Queen's 600 cells; two corners are joined when their cells share a triangle (1200 edges); the five cells round each White Queen edge give a pentagon (720 pentagons); the twenty cells round each White Queen corner give a cell (120 cells). Checked exactly: all corners on one sphere, all edges equal, four edges at every corner, every pentagon regular and shared by exactly two cells, every cell a regular dodecahedron (13 of 13 checks).

Layers and core. Round one dodecahedron the 120 cells stand in nine layers of 1, 12, 20, 12, 30, 12, 20, 12 and 1, each one kind of piece in the perspective view, and the 600 corners in fifteen layers of 20, 20, 30, 60, 60, 60, 20, 60, 20, 60, 60, 60, 30, 20 and 20: exactly the Cleo shells of the White Queen, since her cell centres are the Red Queen's corners. Both lists are palindromes, the same forwards and backwards, as the White Queen's shells are (#63): the Red Queen is centrally symmetric, so the layers counted from one cell match, in reverse, the layers counted from the opposite cell. The two cores exchange one for one: each of the White Queen's twenty Cleo-Icos becomes one of the twenty corners of the Red Queen's central dodecahedron, and each of the twelve corners of the White Queen's icosahedron becomes one of its twelve pentagons. The White Queen's twenty Cleo-Icos are real cells, the shadows of the twenty tetrahedra at her corner. The Red Queen's core is one whole cell; its twelve pentagonal pyramids, Aristotle's dodecahedron pyramid, appear when she is divided from her centre into 120 four-dimensional pyramids, one over each cell: the twelve sides of the central one cast them exactly (§3). In the star Red Queen (§5) they are parts of real cells again. The 15 planes that cut the sectors round a corner (Plates 14 and 35) are mirrors of both Queens, so the sectors are true symmetric pieces.

Ten White Queens inside. The Red Queen also carries White Queens on her own corners. Exactly ten 600-cells are inscribed in her, and every corner lies in exactly two. They are taken five at a time in two ways: each of two states is five disjoint 600-cells that together use all 600 corners once, the two 600-cells through a corner belong to different states, and two from different states meet in exactly 24 corners, a 24-cell (the compound of ten 600-cells is Coxeter's; the states, the counts and the meetings are #110, Theorem B, DICTO DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24272477). They use her corners but not her edges: the edges of an inscribed 600-cell are diagonals of the Red Queen, so none of them makes her pentagons. She is the White Queen's dual, not a union of White Queens; Chapter 10 follows the ones inside her.

How it closes. Three regular dodecahedra round an edge fill 3 × 116.57° = 349.7° and leave a gap of 10.3°, so dodecahedra cannot close round an edge in ordinary space; five regular tetrahedra leave 7.36°. As three pentagons round a corner close only by folding up into the dodecahedron, these gaps close by bending into the fourth dimension, and the Red Queen closes with three dodecahedra round every edge. In the perspective view the pieces close with no gaps and no overlaps, slightly bent, and the last dodecahedron becomes the outside, as the last pentagon of a flattened dodecahedron becomes its outer rim.

3. Main theorem: Aristotle's five pyramids

Theorem. In perspective from just beyond the far side, along a corner or along a cell centre:

(1) Corner cores. The cells at one corner of the 5-cell, the 16-cell and the 600-cell cast the face pyramids of the regular tetrahedron (4), octahedron (8) and icosahedron (20, the Cleo-Icos), apex at the centre.

(2) Cell cores. The sides of the four-dimensional pyramid from the solid's centre over one cell cast the face pyramids of that cell: tetrahedron (5-cell, 16-cell, 600-cell), cube (tesseract, 6), octahedron (24-cell, 8) and dodecahedron (120-cell, 12).

(3) Exact proportions. In every case the shadow is exactly Aristotle's face pyramid of that Platonic solid: its side edge² over its base edge² is 3/8, 1/2, 3/4, (5 + √5)/8 and (9 + 3√5)/8 for the tetrahedron, octahedron, cube, icosahedron and dodecahedron.

(4) Polar pairs. 16-cell (8 octahedron pyramids) ↔ tesseract (6 cube pyramids); 600-cell (20 Cleo-Icos) ↔ 120-cell (12 pentagonal pyramids); the 5-cell and the 24-cell are their own polars.

Proof. The six solids are built exactly (the 5-cell in the hyperplane of five coordinates summing to zero; the 16-cell, tesseract and 24-cell from their standard coordinates; the 600-cell and 120-cell as in §2). For each we take the eye on the axis just beyond the far side and project. On the axis the corner, or the cell centre and the solid's centre, fall on the centre of the picture, so every piece through it becomes a pyramid with its apex there; we check exactly that the far ends of these pieces form a regular solid centred on the picture's centre, count its faces, and compare side edge² over base edge² with Aristotle's pyramid. All ten cases agree exactly (10 of 10).

4. True pieces and their shadows

DICTO pointed out that the pyramids in four dimensions may have different proportions from their shadows. They do. The corner cores are regular tetrahedra in four dimensions, side edge equal to base edge, but their shadows are the flatter face pyramids. The sides of a cell core run from the solid's centre to its corners, so in four dimensions their side edge² over base edge² is the solid's radius² over edge²: 2/5 (5-cell), 1/2 (16-cell), 1 (tesseract and 24-cell), (3 + √5)/2 (600-cell) and 7 + 3√5 (120-cell), while their shadows are again Aristotle's. The four-dimensional solids carry tall pieces; their shadows, in our space, are Aristotle's five pyramids.

5. The star core

No convex regular four-dimensional solid has dodecahedra round a corner: that would be {3,5,3}, which never closes and is the infinite icosahedral honeycomb of hyperbolic space. The star polytope {5/2,5,3}, the small stellated 120-cell of Hess, does. It stands on the White Queen's 120 corners, with 1200 edges joining corners one radius apart, 720 pentagrams and 120 small stellated dodecahedra, and round every corner a regular dodecahedron of twelve cells. Each of these twelve cells meets the corner with one spike, a pentagonal pyramid whose side edge is exactly φ times its base edge. In perspective the twelve spikes cast exactly the dodecahedron cut into Aristotle's twelve pentagonal pyramids (checked exactly, side edge² over base edge² = (9 + 3√5)/8). So the dodecahedral pyramids that no convex solid can hold at a corner sit at the corner of the star Red Queen: golden in four dimensions, Aristotle's in their shadow.

6. The Red Queen's atlas

As for the White Queen in #63, we cut the Red Queen in her four main directions, through a corner, an edge, a pentagon and a cell, from the centre to the tip, at every corner height and once inside every gap, exactly in ℚ(√5). The one-pattern-per-gap lemma of #63 holds here too (cuts at ¼, ½ and ¾ of every gap cross the same edges), so this list is complete. Her corners are the centres of the White Queen's cells in the coordinates of #63 (corner radius² 7/4 + 3√5/4, edge 1/2). The four directions d are the average of the corners of one element: corner-first d = ((7+3√5)/8, (3+√5)/8, 0, 1/4), a corner; edge-first d = ((7+3√5)/8, (3+√5)/8, 0, 0), the midpoint of an edge; pentagon-first d = ((7+3√5)/8, (5+2√5)/20, (5+3√5)/40, 0), the centre of a pentagon; cell-first d = ((7+3√5)/8, 0, 0, 0), the centre of a cell. They are exactly parallel to the White Queen's cell-, triangle-, edge- and vertex-first directions. Every slice is checked exactly as in #63: repeated points merged by exact comparison, every face plane tested to support the slice by an exact sign in ℚ(√5), every face ordered by an exact two-dimensional hull and turned outward, every edge in exactly two faces, and V − E + F = 2. The atlas adds 17 checks of its own, all passed: among them that each gap gives the same faces when cut at ¼, ½ and ¾ of its height, that inside a gap each slice face is the cross-section of exactly one dodecahedron, that every cut point is a corner of its slice, that each shadow is tiled exactly once by its front cells and once by its back cells (exact volume), and that the polar pairings with the White Queen hold.

Atlas. The Red Queen has 124 slice patterns: 31 corner-first (faces of 3 to 8 sides, largest 108 vertices), 45 edge-first (3 to 9, the only nine-sided faces, largest 114), 33 pentagon-first (3 to 8 and 10, largest 120 vertices and 62 faces) and 15 cell-first (faces of 3, 4, 5, 6, 8 and 10 sides, largest 120 vertices).

Cell-first she passes through the Archimedean solids of the icosahedral family, each exact: the rhombicosidodecahedron, the truncated icosahedron and the truncated dodecahedron (each at one height inside its gap), the icosidodecahedron, and regular dodecahedra (edge φ times the cell's) up to the tip, where the slice is the cell itself; these dodecahedra are her only all-pentagon slices. The other directions begin with a regular tetrahedron (corner-first), a regular triangular prism (edge-first) and a regular pentagonal prism (pentagon-first), and regular decagons appear in the pentagon-first middle slice and in one cell-first gap.

Shadows. Her four shadows are

DirectionVertices · edges · facesFacesFront · back · side-on cells
corner-first158 · 240 · 8472 pentagons, 12 decagons54 · 54 · 12
edge-first180 · 282 · 10496 pentagons, 6 decagons, 2 twelve-sided56 · 56 · 8
pentagon-first160 · 250 · 9280 pentagons, 10 octagons, 2 regular decagons54 · 54 · 12
cell-first80 · 120 · 4212 pentagons, 30 hexagons45 · 45 · 30

Every face that is not a pentagon is a side-on cell seen flat. As in the White Queen, each shadow resolves into two exact halves: the front cells fill it exactly once and so do the back cells (exact volume). And the two queens' atlases mirror each other by polarity: each of the Red Queen's shadows is the polar of the White Queen's middle slice in the same direction, and each of her middle slices is the polar of the White Queen's shadow.

7. Prior art and credit

The 120-cell and 600-cell are Schläfli's (about 1852, published 1901); their coordinates, polarity and the star polytopes {5/2,5,3} and the others are in Coxeter's Regular Polytopes, the star polytopes first found by Schläfli and Hess (1883). Alicia Boole Stott, in "On certain series of sections of the regular four-dimensional hypersolids" (Verhandelingen der Koninklijke Akademie van Wetenschappen te Amsterdam, Eerste Sectie, 7, no. 3, 1900), cut the 120-cell cell-first, parallel to one bounding dodecahedron, through the layers of corners to the middle, and drew each section as a flat plan with coloured faces (her diagrams VIII–XIV); we read her paper for this version. Her series matches our eight cell-first cuts through corner layers, from the cell to the centre, among them the rhombicosidodecahedron and the icosidodecahedron, which are hers. The truncated icosahedron and the truncated dodecahedron lie inside gaps between corner layers, which her series does not cut, and she has no corner-, edge- or pentagon-first series and no shadows. The layers 1, 12, 20, 12, 30, … of the 120-cell's cells round one cell are known, for example in Schleimer and Segerman's Puzzling the 120-cell. Aristotle's claim and the long dispute over how many pyramids meet round a point are traced by Lagarias and Zong (2012); the face pyramids of the Platonic solids and which of them fill space are #44, Aristotle's Pyramid, and the Cleo-Icos is #19. We found no earlier statement that Aristotle's five pyramids are exactly the perspective cores of the regular four-dimensional solids, nor of the star core of {5/2,5,3}.

8. Conclusion

Aristotle's five pyramids are the shadows of the four-dimensional regular solids' cores: three as the cells round a corner, three as the sides of the pyramid over a cell, each with exactly Aristotle's proportions, paired by polarity. The Red Queen, the White Queen's dual, holds the dodecahedral one at her core and ten White Queens on her corners, and the star Red Queen holds it at every corner, golden in four dimensions. Her atlas of 124 slices and four shadows mirrors the White Queen's by polarity, shadow for middle slice.

V, E, F count corners, edges and faces; faces are given as number × number of sides; the last column names the uniform solids.
