V1 = 2V2 = 3V3 = 4sV4 = 5V5 = 6V6 = 3′ + 4
V7 = 2′ + 6V8 = 4 + 5V9 = 4s + 6V10 = 3 + 3′ + 5V11 = 2 + 4s + 6V12 = 1 + 3 + 4 + 5

Proof of Proposition 3. (a) In G+, a(x0σ)a−1 = a x0 σ(a)−1·σ, so twisted classes of 2I are the G+-classes in the coset 2I·σ. A σ-stable π extends to π̂ on G+ with π̂(σ) = Jπ−1 after scaling (σ is an involution, so Jπ² is a scalar, fixed to 1 up to sign), and then χπ̂(x0σ) = tr(π(x0) Jπ−1) = χ̃π(x0). The faithful extensions are spin characters of the double cover G+ of S5; by their degrees and their nonzero values (Table A.1) they are ⟨3, 2⟩ (degree 4) and ⟨4, 1⟩ (degree 6). (b) Character values are algebraic integers. The restriction of 6 to 2I has a rational character, so every Galois conjugate of χ̃6 is ±χ̃6 and u, v are rational algebraic integers, hence whole numbers ≥ 0. Spin characters vanish on R (−1 acts as −1 and R = −R). Orthogonality on the outer coset gives 40u + 60v = 120. The only solutions are (0, 2) and (3, 0); the same holds for 4s with u′ = 3 − u, v′ = 2 − v. (c) is the identity argument above, or Schur's rule [2]. (d) An irreducible character of p-defect zero vanishes on all p-singular elements [3]; 3 is the full power of 3 in 240 and divides 6. The last sentence is checked exactly: Φ(x) = φ²·x̄ permutes the 600-cell, sends each cosine c to its conjugate, and fixes each of the five orbits setwise, so the tones are fixed by Galois conjugation and are rational; the controls in §6 confirm it forces no zero. ∎

Proof of Theorem 4. The kernel Nk = {rational weightings w : the degree-k harmonic part of w vanishes} was computed exactly for k ≤ 20 on the 600-cell (python-flint), and the inclusions N7 ⊂ N1 ∩ N5, N9 ⊂ N3 ∩ N5 ∩ N15, N15 ⊂ N3 ∩ N5 ∩ N9 hold exactly (also T6 ⇒ T2, T8 ⇒ T4, T10 ⇒ T2, T4). The proof: Tk = Σρ m(k, ρ) Fρ with Fρ(w) = Σx,y wxwy χρ(xy−1) ≥ 0 (checked exactly on every orbit for k ≤ 30), so Tk(w) = 0 exactly when Fρ(w) = 0 for every constituent ρ of Vk. V5 = 6 ⊂ V7 = 2′ + 6, and V9 = 4s + 6 and V15 = 4s + 2·6 have the same constituents, which gives the first two statements for every real w. For rational w, F2′(w) is the Galois conjugate of F2(w), so F2′(w) = 0 forces T1(w) = F2(w) = 0. ∎

Proof of Proposition 7. On S³ the function x ↦ Uj(⟨x, u⟩) is the reproducing kernel of the degree-j harmonics up to a constant, so a set silent in degree j has Σx∈S Uj(⟨x, u⟩) = 0 for every u. Expand tk in Chebyshev polynomials of the second kind: t³ = (U3 + 2U1)/8, and the U3-coefficients of t³, t⁵, t⁷ are 1/8, 1/8 and 7/64 (exact; scripts/moment_check.py). With degrees 1, 5, 7 silent, only the U3 terms survive in p3, p5, p7, and their ratios are 1 and 7/8. ∎ The check script also confirms the identities on X in 400 random directions (largest deviation 1.3·10−15).

Proofs of Propositions 5 and 6 are given in the statements, with the following details. Proposition 5: odd k is silent exactly when Vk has no 2 or 2′, even k exactly when Vk is a multiple of 5. By Klein's form (proof of Theorem 2) a constituent present in degree k stays present in degree k + 12; 2 or 2′ occurs at k = 1, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19, 21 and 27, which meets every odd residue class mod 12, and a constituent other than 5 occurs at every even k from 6 to 16, so no silence beyond 3, 4, 5, 9 and 15 is possible. Direct exact sums agree on all six pairs of 12-point classes for k ≤ 20 (study) and k ≤ 60 (second implementation); the character values of 2I are the standard table, quoted. For Proposition 6: in (a), σ is conjugation by an element n of the normaliser and a x0 σ(a)−1 = (a(x0n)a−1)n−1; in (b), ρ∘σ = λ·nρn−1 for some n, so a x σ(a)−1 n = λ(a)−1 a(x0n)a−1 and tr(yn) takes the two values ±tr(x0n) on the class: two parallel hyperplanes with normal n−1 (for 2O, where n = 1, tr(y) itself). In (c), by Franzsen's classification [18, Propositions 2.4, 2.8–2.11 and 2.13], every automorphism of a finite irreducible Coxeter group is, up to an inner one, an automorphism that sends reflections to reflections followed by a central twist w ↦ w·w0λ(w) (λ a homomorphism to ℤ/2, w0 = −1), with one exception, the outer automorphism ξ of W(A5) = Sym6. A central twist replaces ρ∘σ by (ρ∘σ) ⊗ λ′ for a sign character λ′, which does not change the case. If σ sends reflections to reflections, ρ∘σ is again a faithful representation in which the simple reflections act as reflections; for the Weyl groups, whose characters are rational, this puts ρ∘σ in case (a) or (b), and for I2(n), H3 and H4 it is a Galois conjugate of ρ, possibly times the sign. This step is not proved in general here; it was checked by computing every automorphism of W(H3) (|Out| = 2), W(H4) (|Out| = 4) and I2(n) for n ≤ 40, and those of the Weyl groups of rank 3 and 4, with exact characters (studies/2026-11-17-independent-checks/109-prop6). For I2(n), with n even, ρm ≅ ρ1 ⊗ λ when m ≡ ±(1 + n/2) mod n, so every Galois twist of I2(8) and I2(12) falls under (b), and a twist outside (a) and (b) exists exactly for n = 5, 7, 9, 10, 11 and n ≥ 13. The exception ξ sends transpositions to triple transpositions, and ρ∘ξ has the value −1 on transpositions, where ρ has 3 and ρ ⊗ sign has −3; so ξ is in case (c) without Galois conjugation. Here ρ ⊗ (ρ∘ξ) = χ[3,2,1] + χ[2,2,1,1] (Σχ²/|G| = 2) has no one-dimensional constituent, and the five twisted classes in ρ(Sym6) ⊂ S²⁴, of sizes 180, 180, 180, 144 and 36, kill exactly degree 1 for k ≤ 40, the only degree in which the twist group has no invariant harmonic (exact; scripts/s6_twist.py, data/s6_twist.log). For 2Dn, ρ1 ⊗ ρm = ρ1+m ⊕ ρ1−m is reducible and every class lies on one great circle (checked for n ≤ 30). The counts Σχ²/|G| were computed exactly.

5. The two silent solids

The hulls of X and F are two golden relatives of uniform 5-cell polytopes (exact, in Q(√5)). The Golden Truncated 5-cell (solid A), the hull of X, has 20 corners, 40 edges, 30 polygons and 10 cells: 5 regular tetrahedra and 5 golden truncated tetrahedra, whose hexagons have sides √2 and φ√2 in turn. It has the face lattice of the truncated 5-cell but two edge lengths, √2 and φ√2, in ratio φ. The Golden Cantellated 5-cell (solid B), the hull of F, has 30 corners, 90 edges, 80 polygons and 20 cells: 5 regular octahedra, 10 golden triangular prisms (side faces golden rectangles √2 × φ√2) and 5 golden cuboctahedra (4 triangles of side √2, 4 of side φ√2 and 6 golden rectangles). It has the face lattice of the cantellated 5-cell, again with the two edge lengths √2 and φ√2. Koca et al. give both as W(A4) polytopes by their Dynkin labels, (τ, 1, 0, 0) and (1, 0, τ, 0) [4, §4.1, eq. (30)], which fix these combinatorial types and the golden edge ratio; the cell, edge and face counts are worked out here. Their Vacuus (the central plates of their faces, in the sense of #105 [27]) is the subject of Appendix B (#110), and the solids are shown in Chapter 11.

Remark (where the sets lie). The other silence sets of the census (§6) were tested against the 600-cell's 60 mirrors and 300 cross walls (central sections parallel to a pair of opposite cells): the 30-point sets of the 600-cell have every central plate on a wall, the 60-point sets too, as most 60-point subsets of the 600-cell do, and the 240-point sets of the 120-cell do not (#110; Appendix B, §8). All five twisted classes of the 600-cell have every plate on a wall, while four of them (X, −X, F, −F) are of the non-heart kind and one (R) is of the heart kind, so the walls do not see the mechanism of twisted silence.

6. The census of hidden zeros

To see whether anything else in the H4 world is silent for a hidden reason, we took five polytopes: the 600-cell, the 120-cell, the 24-cell, the snub 24-cell and the 240 roots of E8 (the units and φ−1 times the units, with the rational form of Conway and Sloane [15, ch. 8]). In each we took every orbit of every listed subgroup class of the symmetry group (every reflection, cyclic and two-generated subgroup up to conjugacy, the quaternion families, the diagonal and Galois-twisted diagonal groups, closed under rotation parts and −I), every union of two orbits of a subgroup with at most 8 orbits, and every union of twisted classes (all unions when a subgroup has at most 9 twisted classes, otherwise unions of up to 3). That gives 3232 distinct sets up to symmetry. Their tones T1 … T20 were computed exactly, and each zero was tested against, in order: no invariant of the set's stabiliser (Molien), none of its full symmetry group, a single latitude with Uk(cos θ) = 0 where the zonal harmonic is the only invariant, twisted silence of a single class, the exact rational span of explained-silent sets and the parity relations, the same span closed under Double Vision, and silence of every invariant weighting of the whole polytope. Controls: 6 controls and 80 in-run soundness checks, 0 failed; 3000 random subsets of the 600-cell showed no exact zero; a fake Double Vision (a random permutation) in the span closure was caught every time. The census is exhaustive over these subgroup-orbit and union families, not over all point subsets of the five polytopes.

Table A.3. Census of 3232 point sets (exact, k ≤ 20). "One condition": the zero puts a single rational linear condition on the stabiliser-invariant weightings.
polytopesetszeros not explained by any tested mechanism
600-cell808degree 8: 30- and 60-point unions of [C3 × C5], [C5 × C5], [C3 × C10] and [C5 × C5].2 orbits; one condition each

What the census settles, at the strength of its study:

  • Single twisted classes with an unforced silence. In 2I under its outer automorphism, only the golden pair (X with 5, 7; F with 3, 6); X also lies in the snub 24-cell, since it avoids 2T. Two small classes from outer automorphisms of 2T and 2O also carry an unforced silence: a 6-point class in the snub 24-cell (degree 3) and an 8-point class in the 24-cell (degree 2). By Proposition 6 they are of types (a) and (b), a hyperplane slice and a latitude pair; the census's automatic latitude test did not flag them, and twisted silence is what clears them there.
  • Ordinary classes of 2I are latitude slices: the 12-point classes (cosine ±φ/2 and ±1/(2φ)) kill exactly degree 4, the 20-point classes (cosine ±1/2) exactly 2 and 5, the 30-point equator every odd degree. These are the classical Chebyshev zeros Uk(cos θ) = sin((k+1)θ)/sin θ = 0, with θ a multiple of π/5, π/3 and π/2. A Chebyshev zero kills only the zonal harmonic about the axis, so it gives silence only while the zonal harmonic is the class's only invariant of that degree; for the icosahedral stabiliser this holds up to degree 5 (its first non-constant invariant on the 3-space has degree 6). That is why Uk(cos 36°) = 0 at k = 9, 14, … and Uk(cos 60°) = 0 at k = 8, 11, … give no silence. Theorem 1 with σ the identity explains the same zeros exactly: V4 = 5 has 5-defect zero, V2 = 3 and V5 = 6 have 3-defect zero.
  • The 120-cell lead. Four 60-point orbits of A5 (the rotation part of W(A4)) in the 120-cell kill degree 4, which A5 does not force. They are one zero, not four (the odd elements of the normaliser ±S5 permute them, acting with sign +1 on the invariant), and each is a rational combination of icosahedral latitude rings at 36° and 72°, which kill degree 4 because sin 5θ = 0: the H4-images of these rings span the whole explained space (rank 525), and the orbits lie in it (exact). The lead is the classical latitude law carried by linearity.
  • E8. The degree-4 harmonic invariant of H4 acting on ℝ⁸ = ℝ⁴ ⊕ ℝ⁴ is the bizonal h = |x∥|⁴ − 3|x∥|²|x⊥|² + |x⊥|⁴, equal on S⁷ to 5s² − 5s + 1 with s = |x∥|². Its roots s = (1 ± 1/√5)/2 are exactly the parallel shares of the two 600-cell shells of E8, so h vanishes on every root and each 600-cell shell is silent in degree 4 (exact). This is the Double Vision split of E8 seen by a harmonic.
  • Unions of several twisted classes (68 sets in the 600-cell, 6 in the 24-cell, 7 in the snub 24-cell, degrees 2 to 7, 9 and 15) are explained by spans of silent parts, except the class pair of Proposition 5 and the seven unions built from it, which account for every 9/15 zero in the 600-cell and the snub 24-cell.

Signatures across groups. Table A.4 lists the silence signatures (k ≤ 30) of twisted and ordinary classes in the families of Proposition 6. Exact direct sums to degree 40 for the binary groups; the criterion of Theorem 1 agreed with the direct sums on all 38 classes of 2T, 2O and 2I, and on every dihedral class tested (n = 5, 7, 8, 9, 10, 12, 15, 16, 18, 20, 24, 30, every twist; wrong twists gave 552 mismatches).

The D16 and D24 rows are the groups study's lists rewritten as complements; all other classes of W(H3), W⁺(H4) and W(H4) show only forced or antipodal silences (full per-class lists in the study logs, lines SIG). The W(H4) classes on S¹⁵ are products O1 × O2 of classes of 2I (twisted × twisted for "Galois both", twisted × ordinary for "left only"). An entry of the matrix x ↦ a x b̄ is bilinear in (a, b), so a degree-k harmonic on ℝ¹⁶ restricts to a sum of products f(a)g(b) of harmonics of degrees j, l ≤ k with j ≡ l ≡ k mod 2, and the sum over O1 × O2 splits as (ΣO1 f)(ΣO2 g). For k = 3 the pairs (j, l) are (1, 1), (1, 3), (3, 1) and (3, 3), and all vanish when one factor is F (silent in 1 and 3) and the other is silent in 1. So these silences follow from the Main theorem; W(H4) on S¹⁵ carries no silence beyond those of 2I. The rule "silent in k exactly when every (j, l) ≠ (0, 0) is killed by one factor" agrees with the direct exact sums on all 70 class products for k ≤ 20 (second implementation); X × X rings in degree 3, as the rule says.

7. Turning the dial

This is DICTO's sonar dial: turn the base point like a dial and watch, degree by degree, which lines vanish. A silence gives a set away to a listener who scans for it. Fix the golden twist, A5 acting on the 4-space {x ∈ ℝ⁵ : Σx = 0} by even permutations, and move the base point. The invariant harmonics hk come exactly from the centred power sums and the Vandermonde, of dimensions 0, 0, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 2, 2, 3, 2, 3 for k = 1, …, 12, matching the harmonic Molien series (1 + t¹⁰)/((1 − t³)(1 − t⁴)(1 − t⁵)). The silence locus of degree k is a surface for k = 3 … 7, a curve for k = 8, 9, 11 and points for k = 10, 12. The base points with nontrivial stabiliser form two circles, C3 = (a, a, a, b, c), which holds X, and C2 = (a, a, b, b, c), which holds F.

  • Edge circles, exact to degree 30. On C3 the special points are X and −X (minimal polynomial s² − 3s + 1; signature 1, 2, 5, 7), the A4 root (antipodal), and irrational points silent in exactly one of 4, 6, 7. On C2: F and −F (s² − 5; signature 1, 2, 3, 6), the antipodal point, and irrational points silent in exactly one of 4, 6, 7. Degrees 3 and 5 and every degree ≥ 8 are silent on the circles only at the twisted classes and the antipodal points (Figure A.5).
  • Faces and interior, degrees ≤ 12, certified. A Newton search from 300 starts located 259 roots: 55 Krawczyk-certified isolated points (47 distinct orbits), 105 on lower strata, 97 on the antipodal circle (a, b, 0, −b, −a), and 2 false convergences rejected by the direct sums. Every certified generic orbit carries exactly its defining silences and no extra one. The pairs (3, 5), (3, 6), (3, 7) and (5, 7) occur only at lower-strata points: X, F and the roots. The search is not exhaustive.
  • Ordinary control. Ordinary classes of 2I are latitude slices, and there multi-silence comes in families: at cosine ±1/2 every axis, generic ones included, gives a 60-point orbit silent in 2 and 5, a whole 2-dimensional family. The twisted dial gives multi-silence only at isolated, overdetermined points, and those points are the 600-cell classes.

The inverse question. Which sets have a prescribed signature? This is the inverse problem for harmonic strength; on the circle Misawa and Nishimura show that every finite set of degrees is the exact signature of some finite set [40], and on Sd, d ≥ 2, that an infinite signature forces antipodality [39]. For the orbits used here the answer is closed-form: a dihedral orbit {±α + 2πj/N} is silent in {k : N ∤ k} together with {k = cN : cos(cNα) = 0}, so extra silences must form one odd progression. For the golden twist, the signature {1, 2, 5, 7} is realised by X and −X only (exact on the edges, numerical elsewhere), and {1, 2, 3, 6} by F and −F only. Two-degree signatures {1, 2, a, b} with a, b between 3 and 11 occur at isolated certified 60-point orbits, for the pairs (3, 4), (4, 5), (4, 6), (4, 7), (5, 6), (6, 7) on the faces and (4, 9), (4, 11), (5, 8), (5, 9), (5, 11), (6, 8), (6, 9), (6, 11), (7, 8), (7, 9) in the interior.

8. A crystallographic analogy

What is proved here is representation theory: the silences are statements about sums of matrix coefficients over finite subsets of the group SU(2). Reading them as a crystallographic mechanism is an analogy, and no physical extinction rule is claimed. The direct test of a physical reading failed, and we report it first.

No extinct Bragg peaks. Silent degrees do not make extinct peaks in a quasicrystal decorated by the set. Decorating every point of a golden cut-and-project module (a stand-in for the Fourier module of the H4 quasicrystal of Elser and Sloane [25], window |m| ≤ 6, |m★| ≤ 1.5) by X, −X, F or the control set gives no zero among 16 488 peaks (smallest |F|/|S| of order 10−3; numerical pilot). The antipodal R shows 72 zeros, from antipodality, not from silence. A silent degree removes one angular component (one multipole) of the motif's form factor at every |k| without forcing the full amplitude to vanish anywhere. What it does leave is a rigidity of the odd part, which Proposition 7 makes exact: on X the odd moments of order 5 and 7 are fixed multiples of the order-3 moment, so Im FX(k) keeps the shape of a degree-3 harmonic up to order |k|⁸. Galois conjugation sits at the same structural place in both subjects, the twisted diagonal g ↦ (g, g★), but the silence lives in the group's Fourier transform, not in the translation Fourier transform of the quasicrystal.

The analogy. Treat a point set on S³ as a crystal in the group SU(2). Its Bragg peaks are the Peter–Weyl blocks End(Vk), one per degree, and its tone in degree k is its Fourier content there. The twisted action x ↦ a x σ(a)−1 is a translation by t(a) = a σ(a)−1 followed by a point operation. In a crystal, a peak is extinct when its little group acts on the one-dimensional Fourier space with a nontrivial phase (Mermin's rule [19]; extinctions as group cohomology, Rabson and Fisher [20]; Baake and Grimm call such a zero an extinction [23]). On S³ a block is extinct when the symmetry has no invariant vector on it, that is, Hom(π∘σ, π) = 0: the same rule with a matrix in place of the phase (Schur's lemma).

Proposition 8 (forced silences as a group-theoretic analogue of systematic absences). Exact for the first two sentences; the last is a naming by analogy. Let G ⊂ SU(2) be finite and nonabelian and σ an automorphism of G. The twisted action of G on S³ fixes a point if and only if σ is conjugation by an element of SU(2), and this holds if and only if degree 1 is not forced silent on twisted classes. For 2I and its Galois-type σ, no point is fixed and the action is nonsymmorphic; we read the forced silences 1 and 2 of every twisted class as the group-theoretic analogue of its systematic absences.

Proof. x is fixed when a x σ(a)−1 = x for all a, that is σ(a) = x−1 a x. Degree 1 is V1 = ρ, the defining representation, irreducible since G is nonabelian; it is forced silent exactly when ρ is not σ-stable (Theorem 1, every class), and ρ∘σ ≅ ρ means ρ∘σ = u ρ u−1 for some unitary u, which can be scaled into SU(2). For 2I, V1 = 2 and V2 = 3 are exchanged by σ with 2′ and 3′. ∎ The crystal analogue is weaker: the nonsymmorphic groups I212121 and I213 show no systematic extinction [20].

The new silences and special positions. Degrees 5 and 7 of X are not forced by its symmetry group or its space group: a different X-shaped S5-orbit, the permutations of (1.7, 1.7, 1.7, 0.6, 0), kills only 1 and 2. Their nearest crystallographic counterpart, again by analogy, is the special-position (Wyckoff) reflection condition, in which atoms on a special site cancel reflections the space group allows; the textbook case is diamond's 222 reflection, cancelled by atoms on the site 8a of Fd3̄m [21]. In both cases the cancellation comes from where the generating point sits. Proposition 3 (d) makes this precise for X: the 6-block has 3-defect zero, X sits over an element of order 6, and the zero is a cancellation over the order-3 part of X's site. F's zero has no such reading.

9. A fourth kind of forced law

The law-giving census of the Wythoff families (all one-parameter edge-length families of the reflection groups A3, B3, H3, A4, B4, D4, F4 and H4) knows three ways in which a law is forced at a member: (P) parity, a quantity odd under an involution of the family vanishes at its fixed point; (E) evenness, an even quantity is critical there; (X) extra symmetry, the member has symmetry its neighbours lack. A normaliser argument shows that in the seed space of a finite reflection group every similarity between members comes from a diagram automorphism, so no new law of these kinds hides off the automorphism-fixed locus. The census found 416 coincidences; all but two were explained by (X) and (E) at t = 1. The two left were the golden A4 members X and F, edge members of two Wythoff families of A4, each silent in two degrees on a one-parameter edge, with symmetry exactly S5.

The Main theorem shows they are forced, by none of (P), (E), (X). We call this kind (T), twisted silence (DICTO's name), a fourth kind identified within the scope of the census: the corners form a twisted conjugacy class of a finite group whose elements are the corners of a polytope, and a tone vanishes because a twisted character is forced to vanish there. The normaliser theorem is untouched, because (T) uses no involution of the family and no similarity; Galois maps are not similarities and the normaliser argument cannot see them. The law holds at the member because the member is a 600-cell class: the neighbouring members leave the 600-cell, and the control member t = 2, the permutations of (3, 3, 3, 2, 0), has an infinite reflection closure and only the forced silences 1 and 2. With (T), all 416 coincidences of the census are explained. This classifies the forced laws met in this census; it does not claim that every mechanism for forced laws is now known. Figure A.6 sets the kinds side by side.

A systematic exact search over 820 subgroup orbits in the 600-cell, the 24-cell, the 120-cell and the E8 roots found no other orbit with two silences its symmetry does not force, apart from the two 20-point latitude classes of the 600-cell (silent in 2 and 5 by the Chebyshev rule at θ = π/3, two independent conditions met by one angle). The golden pair is the only case of the kind (T) found there.

10. Open problems

  1. The degree-8 sets. Four classes of 30- and 60-point unions of [Cm × Cn] orbits in the 600-cell, and one 240-point union of [C5 × 2T] orbits in the 120-cell, are silent in degree 8 for no tested reason (Table A.3). They are products L·x·R of two cyclic groups, outside Theorem 1 because the left and right factors are independent. A double-coset explanation, in which the harmonic sum factorises as ρ(L-sum)·ρ(x)·ρ(R-sum), is the natural first try.
  2. The E8 degree-4 unions. The 120-root unions 96 + 24 and 72 + 48 over the two 600-cell shells kill degree 4 with one condition left after the bizonal root.
  3. Exhaustive dial. Prove by elimination that no generic point off the edge circles is silent in (5, 7) or (3, 6), and extend the certified search beyond degree 12.
  4. Special silences of 2I by prime-defect label: a complete list.
  5. Prior art. Found earlier: the forced silences of orbits (Sobolev [33]; Goethals and Seidel, as restated in [34]; Bannai [10]); the notion of a silence signature (spherical T-designs [38], harmonic index [8, 9], designs admitting indices [37], harmonic strength [39]); the S5 split of the 600-cell into 20 + 20 + 20 + 30 + 30 and the description of W(A4) by Galois-conjugate quaternion pairs (Koca, Koç, Al-Barwani and Al-Farsi 2006 [32], then [4]), so the golden twist is implicit there, though not as twisted conjugacy; the ingredients of Theorem 1 (Fel'shtyn and Troitsky [5], Fel'shtyn and Hill [36]); the inverse problem on the circle [40]. Not found in the sources read (Sources checked, at the end of this appendix): the silence theorem for harmonic sums over twisted classes and its use for unforced zeros, the signatures {1, 2, 5, 7} and {1, 2, 3, 6}, the class pair, the degree linkage, the E8 bizonal root, the twist classification read on spheres, the product reading of the W(H4) classes, the golden dial and the reading of forced silences as systematic absences. Goethals and Seidel 1981 [7], §5 (pp. 212–214), read: it treats orbits of the full groups W(A3), W(B3), W(H3) and W(H4) only, with the H4 invariants of degrees 2, 12, 20 and 30, the spherical 19-designs from the zeros of the degree-12 invariant (the smallest has 1440 points), and a cubature of strength 19 from the 600-cell's vertices with the centres of its faces; it has no orbits of S5 or of any other subgroup, and no twisted classes. Coxeter [22], read in the 1948 edition (§§8.2–8.8, pp. 150–161; §§13.4–13.7, pp. 244–251; Tables V–VII, pp. 298–305): Gosset's construction of the 600-cell from the snub 24-cell and 24 icosahedral pyramids, its coordinates as the even permutations of (±τ, ±1, ±τ⁻¹, 0) with the 25 inscribed 24-cells (five disjoint), the polytopes of the 5-cell family as permutations of 1s and 0s (not golden), the parallel sections, facetings and regular compounds, and the triacontagonal projection with the 120 vertices in four rings of 30; nothing on S5-orbits of the 600-cell, golden relatives of the 5-cell family, or harmonic silences.

Appendix B. Walls of Silence: The Walls of the 4D 600-cell and 120-cell

Paper #110 in full: its statements, proofs, tables and checks, from Version 2 (17 November 2026), DICTO DOI 10.5281/zenodo.24272477 (all versions; Version 2: 10.5281/zenodo.24296556). It is retold in Chapter 5, §10, and Chapters 10 and 11, and is given here with the paper named as its source. The theorem and section numbers are the paper's; its figures and its Tables 2 and 3 are in those chapters and are referred to by their book numbers (Figures 5.4, 5.5, 10.1, 10.2 and 11.2 to 11.6; Tables 11.1 and 11.2), and its other tables are numbered B.1 to B.3. Scripts, exact checks and data: https://github.com/DICTOR-Master/kaleidohedra/tree/1c2535a4/studies (research branch, commit 1c2535a4: folders 2026-11-16-600cell-walls, 2026-11-16-non-heart-vacuus, 2026-11-16-queens-vicinity-vacuus, 2026-11-16-queens-chambers, 2026-11-16-queens-construct, 2026-11-16-queens-shadows and 2026-11-16-redqueen-cats; the paper's own checks, scripts/face_lemmas.py and scripts/fig_orbits_dark.py, are in its folder; the second implementation of the exact arguments, with one script to rerun it, is in studies/2026-11-17-independent-checks, folders 110-exact and 110-chambers).

The shapes read here are their finders': each is credited where it is used. The 600-cell and the 120-cell are Schläfli's [3]; the snub 24-cell is Gosset's [4]; the grand antiprism is Conway and Guy's [5]; the special cuts (diminished 600-cells) and their count are Dutour Sikirić and Myrvold's [6]; the split of the 600-cell into W(A4)-orbits of 20, 20, 20, 30 and 30 corners is Koca, Koç, Al-Barwani and Al-Farsi's [7], with the orbits' Dynkin labels in Koca, Ozdes Koca and Al-Ajmi [8]; the 25 inscribed 24-cells are Schoute's, as proved by Denney et al. [9]; the compound of ten 600-cells in the 120-cell is in Coxeter [10]; the 60 and 300 wall directions are the rays of Waegell and Aravind's Kochen–Specker sets [18, 19]; the faces of the Queens on the 60 mirrors are #107's Corollary 2 [16]; the region count of a hyperplane arrangement is Zaslavsky's [12].
DICTO: the title, the opening images (the hidden submarine, the wood and the trees, the cat and the meat, the many half-alive cats), the idea that the silent shapes were hidden because they look like the walls of the 600-cell, the Vacuus and its three kinds (heart, inherited heart, non-heart), the names the Queens, twisted silence, cross walls, Golden Truncated 5-cell and Golden Cantellated 5-cell, the ruling that cross walls count, the re-reading of the shapes round the Queens as Vacuus, the survey of the 120-cell, the proposal that Vacuus fins form the Queens' skeletons, and the shadows as lattices of possible Vacuus.

Abstract. The 120-cell (Schläfli, 1852) holds ten inscribed 600-cells in two states of five, every corner in exactly two. We show that it also holds exactly 1200 copies of the Golden Truncated 5-cell A, 1200 of the Golden Cantellated 5-cell B and 600 of the expanded 5-cell C3, the three kinds of shape that the 5-cell's symmetry group cuts from a 600-cell, all inside the ten 600-cells. Their walls fall into three classes, of 60, 300 and 1200 hyperplanes: every A copy is hidden in the 120-cell's own walls, while B and C3 copies are hidden only in the walls of their own 600-cell, and in each 600-cell the 120 B copies split its 1200 triangles exactly. The 120-cell's own cats are the polar duals of these: the duals of the five orbits of one S5 intersect exactly in the 120-cell, every plate of every dual lies on its 60 mirrors, and their fins cover all its pentagons and edges. A shape is hidden in a host when every central plate of its Vacuus, the hyperplane through the centre spanned by a two-face, is a wall of the host: a mirror, or a cross wall (a central section parallel to a pair of opposite cells). A and B are the solids of Silent Running [1] (Appendix A), silent in harmonic degrees that their symmetry does not force. Three face lemmas show why they are hidden in the 600-cell: every triangle of its edges and every golden rectangle on its corners lies on a mirror, and every triangle of side equal to its radius lies on a cross wall. An index law reads the three kinds of Vacuus (heart, inherited heart, non-heart) off the symmetry of each plate. The walls cut the 600-cell into 4,291,200 chambers and the 120-cell, with all 1560 walls, into 593,236,800.

1. Hidden in the walls

The images behind this appendix are DICTO's and are told in Chapters 9 and 11: the submarine running silent, the wood that cannot be seen for the trees, and the cat and the meat. The shapes hidden in the walls are the cats (A, B, C3 and their copies, and in the Red Queen their duals), and the walls are the meat. If the shapes hide in the walls, do they make up the solid that has those walls? This appendix makes both questions exact. Theorem A says where A and B hide in the 600-cell and why; Theorem B, the main result, follows them into the 120-cell, where every corner belongs to two whole 600-cells at once; Theorem C counts the chambers that the walls cut.

The 600-cell has 120 corners, 720 edges, 1200 triangles and 600 tetrahedral cells; the 120-cell has 600 corners and 120 dodecahedral cells (Schläfli [3]). In DICTO's books they are the Queens: the White Queen is the 600-cell and the Red Queen the 120-cell.

2. Definitions

The Vacuus of a pair. Let X be a convex polytope and O a point inside it. The ridges of X are its faces of dimension two less than X: edges in three dimensions, two-faces in four. Each ridge f spans with O one hyperplane through O, its plate; the cone from O over f is its fin. The Vacuus of (X, O) is the set of plates, each with the ridges on it. This widens DICTO's Vacuus of #87 [14] (fins from the centre to the edges) and #105 [15] (central slices carrying two-faces) to every polytope with a chosen centre. The centre matters: for a shape built on the corners of a host polytope, O is the host's centre in this appendix. For A, B, C3 and every orbit of a group fixing the centre, this is also the centroid of the shape's corners; for other shapes the two differ, and the kinds and verdicts below can change (Proposition 4, §8).

Sheets and kinds. A plate is a full sheet if with each ridge f it carries the opposite ridge −f (through O), a shared sheet if it carries two or more ridges but is not full, and a single fin if it carries one. (X, O) is of the heart kind when every plate is a full sheet, of the non-heart kind when every plate is a single fin, and of the inherited heart kind otherwise (DICTO's names; Figures 10.1 and 10.2).

Walls of a host. For a host H with centre O, W(H) is its set of walls: its mirror hyperplanes and its cross walls, the hyperplanes through O parallel to a pair of opposite cells that are not already mirrors. A shape on H's corners is hidden in H when every plate of its Vacuus (about O) is in W(H), partly hidden when some are, not hidden when none is. For the 600-cell P, W(P) is 60 mirrors (normals along its corners) and 300 cross walls (normals along its cell centres, the corners of the dual 120-cell): 360 walls. For the dual 120-cell the cell-parallel sections are the 60 mirrors, so the dual pair has one wall system of 360 (exact; the mirrors carry both Queens' faces by #107's Corollary 2 [16]). The 60 and 300 normal directions are the rays of Waegell and Aravind's Kochen–Specker sets from the 600-cell and the 120-cell [18, 19].

Why the cross walls belong to the wall system. The cross walls are not mirrors: no reflection of the 600-cell fixes them. They are nevertheless canonical, for reasons that do not mention A or B. (1) They are defined from the 600-cell's cells as the mirrors are from its corners: the mirrors are the central hyperplanes perpendicular to the 60 corner axes, the cross walls those perpendicular to the 300 cell axes, so each is the central section parallel to a pair of opposite cells, and under duality the two families exchange roles (the 120-cell's cell-parallel sections are the 60 mirrors). (2) The 600-cell holds 25 inscribed 24-cells (Schoute; Denney et al. [9]). The central section of one of them parallel to a pair of its opposite octahedral cells holds 12 corners of the 600-cell, and the 25 × 12 = 300 such sections are distinct and are exactly the cross walls (exact; second implementation, data crosswalls_24cells.csv). So the cross walls are the 24-cells' own middle walls, inherited by the 600-cell. (3) Their 300 normals are the rays of Waegell and Aravind's 120-cell Kochen–Specker set [19]. In the order of discovery, the cross walls were first tested after B's 40 non-mirror plates were found on them (DICTO's ruling, 16 November); every later test in this appendix used the fixed 360.

Coordinates. As in Appendix A, the 600-cell is placed in the sum-zero space of ℝ⁵, where the group S5 of the 5-cell permutes coordinates and φ = (1 + √5)/2. A, the Golden Truncated 5-cell, is the hull of the 20 permutations of (φ², φ², φ², φ, 0), B, the Golden Cantellated 5-cell, of the 30 permutations of (φ², φ², 1, 1, 0), and C3 of the 20 roots of A4, the uniform expanded 5-cell; with −A and −B they are the five S5-orbits of the 600-cell's corners [7, 8]. The 600-cell's edge is √2 and its radius φ√2.

Reproduction. Every exact claim has a script and a log in the folders of the meta line. The exact arguments of Theorems A, B and B′, Lemma 3 and Proposition 5 were redone by a second implementation written separately from the study scripts, and the chamber counts of Theorem C were recomputed from the intersection lattice (Appendix B); one script reruns them. Every number checked agrees, and the W(H4)-transitivity in Lemma 3 is exact. One step in Theorem B′ (d) is floating point: that the regions left uncovered on a plate have zero area (every region of positive area is certified by an exact witness point). Both implementations were written with an AI assistant; no specialist has reviewed the paper, so the results are reproducible, not independently refereed.

3. The kinds from the plates

Lemma 1. Exact. (X, O) is of the heart kind exactly when X is centrally symmetric about O.
Proof. Put O at the origin. If X = −X, then with each ridge f, −f is a ridge, and it lies in the plate span(f) of f; f ≠ −f because f lies in a supporting hyperplane n·x = h with h > 0 and −f in n·x = −h. Conversely, if every plate is a full sheet, the set of ridges is closed under x ↦ −x; every corner of X lies on a ridge, so the corner set and its hull X are too. ∎

Proposition 2 (the index law). Exact. Let G be the symmetry group of X fixing O, f a ridge with plate H, Gf the symmetries keeping f and GH those keeping H. The ridges of the orbit G·f that lie on H number exactly |GH| / |Gf|. So (X, O) is non-heart exactly when GH = Gf for every ridge and no two orbits of ridges share a plate; heart exactly when −1 ∈ G (Lemma 1); and inherited heart otherwise, the shared sheets being the plates whose own symmetry is larger than that of their ridges, or that carry ridges of two orbits.
Proof. g·f lies on H exactly when the plate of g·f, which is g·H, equals H, that is when g ∈ GH; and Gf ⊂ GH, so the orbit of f under GH has |GH| / |Gf| members. ∎

This is the orbit–stabiliser count restated; its use is that the kind is read off each plate. Table B.1 shows it on every shape with a shared or full sheet that we met under S5 (stabilisers computed in floating point in a second program; the counts agree with the exact face counts of the studies).

Table B.1. The index law on S5-orbits (centre = host centre = centroid). For each class of plates under S5: the number of plates, ridges per plate, |GH|, and |Gf| of the ridges on it.
shapeplatesridges per plate|GH||Gf|kind
A20; 101; 16; 126; 12non-heart
B30; 40; 101; 1; 14; 6; 124; 6; 12non-heart
C3 (on the 600-cell)514 = 6 + 4 + 4244 (six squares), 6 (eight triangles)heart
runcitruncated 5-cells on the 120-cell30; 40; 51; 1; 10 = 6 + 44; 6; 244; 6; 4 and 6inherited heart
bitruncated 5-cell on the 120-cell10; 102; 212; 1212 and 12; 6heart
uniform runcitruncated 5-cell; two generic seeds12014 or 64 or 6non-heart
generic expanded 5-cell (3.1, 1, 1, 1, 0)7014 or 64 or 6non-heart

S5 does not contain −1, so a generic S5-orbit is non-heart; a plate gains symmetry only at special seeds. In the three shapes above with shared or full sheets under S5, those plates are the five hyperplanes "coordinate k equals the mean", kept by the 24 permutations of the other coordinates. A ridge lies on one only if all its corners have k-th coordinate equal to the mean, so one coordinate of the seed must equal the mean of the five (C3: (φ, 0, 0, 0, −φ); the two runcitruncated orbits of the 120-cell: means 0.809 and 0.5, each a coordinate). A and B fail this condition and have no shared sheet. Whether the condition is sufficient for every seed pattern is not checked. C3's five plates of 14 faces are the off-mirror slices found by #108 [17], which reports the runcinated 5-cell and the 4D permutohedron as the only polytopes of A4, B4, F4 and H4 with central slices off the mirrors carrying three or more faces.

4. The walls of A and B

Lemma 3 (the face lemmas). On the 600-cell's corners (edge √2, radius φ√2):
(a) every triangle of three edges (1200) lies on a mirror, 20 on each, and every pentagon of the dual 120-cell lies on a mirror, 12 on each (#107, Corollary 2 [16]);
(b) every golden rectangle with sides an edge and a radius (1800) lies on a mirror, 30 on each;
(c) every triangle of side equal to the radius (2400) lies on a cross wall, 8 on each;
(d) a face fixed point by point by a reflection of H4 lies on its mirror.
Proof. (d): the fixed points of a reflection are its mirror, and the plate is the span of the face. For (b) and (c), W(H4) is transitive on each class (exact, in the second implementation), so one face of each settles the wall. As unit quaternions (the 600-cell at radius 1), the radius triangle 1, ½(1, 1, 1, 1), ½(1, 1, 1, −1) spans the hyperplane with normal (0, 1, −1, 0), a cell-centre direction, so it is a cross wall; the rectangle 1, ½(1, 1, 1, 1), ½(φ−1, 0, 1, φ), ½(φ, −1, 0, φ−1) has normal ½(0, −φ−1, φ, −1), a corner, so it is a mirror (both exact by hand). The counts per wall follow from transitivity (also checked in floating point, data/face-lemmas.log, FINAL ok). ∎

Theorem A (the silent solids are hidden in the 600-cell). Exact. Every plate of A and of B is in W(P). A's 30 plates (20 on edge triangles, 10 on hexagons) are mirrors. Of B's 80, the 10 on edge triangles and the 30 on golden rectangles are mirrors, and the 40 on radius triangles are cross walls. A's and B's mirrors share exactly the 10 mirrors of S5, and together they are all 60. C3's 70 faces lie on 5 cross walls, 14 on each. A and B are non-heart.
Proof. A's triangles have side √2 and are edge triangles (Lemma 3 a). Each of A's hexagons is fixed point by point by a transposition of two coordinates, a reflection of H4 (Lemma 3 d). B's √2-triangles, its √2 × φ√2 rectangles and its φ√2-triangles are edge triangles, golden rectangles and radius triangles (Lemma 3 a, b, c). That each plate carries one face, the counts 30 and 80, the shared 10 mirrors and C3's plates are the exact Q(√5) run of the walls study (each plate's normal an exact null vector, matched exactly against the 360 wall normals), which also confirms the rest. Table 11.1 gives the kinds of plate and their slices; Figure 11.2 shows the five orbits. ∎

So A and B are hidden because they are made of the 600-cell's corners with few faces per plate, not because of the twist of Silent Running: the same lemmas hide most symmetric shapes on those corners (§8).

Proposition 4 (the special cuts). Exact. Remove from the 600-cell an independent set C of k corners (no two adjacent) and take the hull of the rest: a special cut of Dutour Sikirić and Myrvold, who count 314,248,344 of them up to symmetry [6]. About the 600-cell's centre, every special cut is hidden in the 60 mirrors; it is of the heart kind when C is antipodal and of the inherited heart kind otherwise. The grand antiprism (Conway and Guy [5]) removes adjacent corners and is not a special cut.
Proof. In [6, §2] the 20 tetrahedra at a removed corner v become one icosahedral cell; so every two-face of the cut is a triangle of the 600-cell, on a mirror (Lemma 3 a). Each corner lies in 30 triangles and no triangle has two corners of C, so 1200 − 30k triangles remain. Each kept corner has at most 3 neighbours in C, since its neighbours form an icosahedron, in which no 4 corners are pairwise non-adjacent; counting edges between C and the rest, 12k ≤ 3(120 − k), so k ≤ 24 (as in [6]) and at least 480 triangles lie on 60 mirrors, so some mirror carries two or more. By Lemma 1 the cut is heart exactly when it is centrally symmetric, that is when C is antipodal. ∎ Example: three corners at 120° to one another on one hexagon of the 600-cell are independent, sum to zero and are not antipodal; removing them leaves 1110 triangles on the 60 mirrors (3 mirrors with 14, 36 with 18, 21 with 20), inherited heart, with the centroid at the centre (floating point, review). Measured from its own centroid instead, the 600-cell less one corner has 915 plates, 900 of them single fins on no wall: the centre must be stated.

5. The cats in the Red Queen

DICTO's image of the two states, Schrödinger's many half-alive cats coexisting, is told in Chapter 11, §4; no physics is claimed. Let Q be the 120-cell and P1, …, P10 its inscribed 600-cells (on its corners, not dual to it). W(Q) is Q's 60 mirrors and the 300 hyperplanes perpendicular to its corner axes (the cross walls of its dual 600-cell).

Theorem B (the cats in the Red Queen). Exact unless marked.
(a) Q has exactly 10 inscribed 600-cells. Every corner lies in exactly 2. They form two states of 5 disjoint 600-cells, and the two through a corner are in different states; two from different states meet in exactly 24 corners, a 24-cell.
(b) Q holds exactly 1200 copies of A (A and −A), 1200 of B (B and −B) and 600 of C3, all inside the Pi, 120, 120 and 60 in each; under Q's symmetry group each is one class, with stabilisers of order 12, 12 and 24.
(c) The walls W(P1) ∪ … ∪ W(P10) are 1560: the 60 mirrors of Q, each a cross wall of all ten; the 300 other walls of Q, each a mirror of exactly the 2 Pi through that corner axis; and 1200 hyperplanes not in W(Q), each a cross wall of exactly 2 Pi. Every A copy is hidden in Q itself: its plates use only the 300. Every B copy and every C3 copy is hidden in its own Pi but not in Q: B copies use all three classes, C3 copies the 60 and the 1200.
(d) In each Pi, every one of its 1200 triangles is a face of exactly one of its 120 B copies and of exactly two of its 120 A copies: the B copies split the triangles exactly.
Proof. (a) Each corner of Q has 24 corners at 36°, which fall into two sets of 12 with no pair at 36° between the sets (floating point, review). An inscribed 600-cell through the corner must contain one whole set, as its 12 neighbours, and each set generates one 600-cell under reflection; so at most 2 pass through each corner, and the compound of ten 600-cells (Coxeter [10, Table VII]; Denney et al. [9]) has 2 through each, since 10 × 120 = 2 × 600. Each was checked to be exactly closed under its own reflections; the states, the 24-corner meetings and the disjointness within a state are exact checks. (b) Inside one 600-cell the subgroups S5 that cut it into A, −A, C3, B, −B are exactly the 60 generated by an A4 set of its reflections, all conjugate (normaliser of order 240), each giving one copy of each orbit. The search over all of Q's corners first filters by distances in floating point (tolerance 10−6, which can only admit too many) and then checks each candidate's full Gram matrix exactly, so it is complete; it finds no A or B copy outside the Pi. The stabiliser orders give 14400/12 = 1200 and 14400/24 = 600. (c) Theorem A carried into each Pi by an exact similarity, checked directly on one A and one B copy in each; the classes of the 1560 walls and their hosts are exact checks, recounted in floating point by a second program and in exact arithmetic by the second implementation. (d) Each A copy has 20 of the 600-cell's triangles as faces (its 5 tetrahedra are cells of the 600-cell) and each B copy 10 (its edge triangles, Table 11.1); the 600-cell's symmetry group is transitive on its triangles and on each family of copies, so each triangle is a face of 120 × 20 / 1200 = 2 A copies and 120 × 10 / 1200 = 1 B copy. ∎

Proposition 5 (half-cats). Exact computation; the full enumeration is in the supporting data (all 90,000 cases and the 18 patterns, second implementation, data prop5_cases.csv.gz and prop5_patterns.txt). Fix an S5 in a 600-cell of the first state. Of its 5 shared 24-cells, one is split 0 A, 12 C3, 12 B and the other four 10 A, 2 C3, 12 B; so every shared 24-cell is half B in each of its two 600-cells, whatever S5 is chosen. Over all pairs of choices (25 × 60 × 60 = 90,000 cases) the corners of a shared 24-cell fall into 18 patterns of (orbit in the first state, orbit in the second); in 1500 cases (1 in 60) every corner is in the same orbit in both. No copy lies in both states (the 600-cells of a state are disjoint), but A and B copies from the two states share at most 6 corners and always share walls (two C3 copies can share 12 corners and no wall): two A copies at least 2, an A and a B copy at least 1, two B copies at least 5.

The dual cats. DICTO's proposal of 16 November, which found them, is told in Chapter 11, §5: the premise at fault was inclusion. An inscribed 600-cell uses the 120-cell's corners, but its edges are diagonals of the 120-cell, so its copies never make the 120-cell's edges or pentagons. The two Queens are related by duality, and DICTO's hypothesis, confirmed below, is that the Red Queen's own cats are the polar duals of the White Queen's. Let P be the 600-cell dual to Q (its corners along Q's cell centres), take polarity about the common centre with r² = |corner of Q|², and write X* = {y : x·y ≤ r² for every corner x of X}.

Theorem B′ (the dual cats). Exact in Q(√5) unless marked.
(a) P* = Q and Q* = P. For each of P's 60 S5s, Q = A* ∩ (−A)* ∩ C3* ∩ B* ∩ (−B)*, and the five duals use Q's 120 cell walls once each.
(b) A* has 10 corners, 30 edges, 40 triangles and 20 tetrahedral cells: the 5-cell with a low pyramid on each cell, its Kleetope (the polar of a truncation is the Kleetope of the polar; Grünbaum [21]); 5 of its corners are corners of Q. B* has (20, 80, 90, 30), with 30 triangular bipyramids as cells; C3* has (30, 70, 60, 20), with 20 cells of 8 corners and 6 quadrilaterals (trigonal-trapezohedron type). No names for these three duals were found in the sources checked.
(c) Every plate of every dual copy lies on one of Q's own 60 mirrors, 10 per copy (the mirrors of its S5), and none on the 300 or the 1200 other walls. A* (4 faces on each plate) and B* (9) are of the inherited heart kind; C3* (6) is heart.
(d) The fins of the 120 A* copies, and those of the 120 B* copies, each cover all 720 pentagons and all 1200 edges of Q whole, cut off at their faces (each dual contains Q, so finite and radial fins agree): every pentagon is covered by 10 A*, 10 B* and 5 C3* copies, and each copy covers a pentagon whole or not at all, 60 pentagons in all (whole by the union of that copy's fins on the pentagon's plate; a single fin of B* holds only some of them, of C3* none). The fewest copies that cover every pentagon are 16 A*, 16 B* or 22 C3* (each cover re-checked as a set union; optimality certified: no cover of 15, 15 or 21 copies exists, by DRAT proofs from the SAT solver CaDiCaL checked by the independent proof checker drat-trim, without symmetry breaking (the solvers HiGHS and CP-SAT agree); the covers, the instance, the formulas and the check logs are in the supporting data. No short hand-checkable bound reaches the optimum: the linear relaxation gives 12, counting over mirrors 12, 12 and 18); no selection of dual copies covers each pentagon exactly once (complete exact-cover search), so there is no counterpart of Theorem B (d).
(e) Dualising the inclusion copies of Theorem B instead (the polars of all 3000 copies in the ten inscribed 600-cells) puts every fin plate on the 300 corner-axis walls of Q and covers no pentagon.
(f) Each corner of Q is a corner of exactly one A* copy: the 120 A* copies split Q's 600 corners into 120 sets of 5, one corner from each of the ten inscribed 600-cells. One level down the two states return: P splits into 5 disjoint 24-cells in 10 ways, each of its 25 24-cells lies in exactly 2 of the splits, one from each of two states of 5, and one S5 cuts every split as (A, C3, B) = (0, 12, 12) once and (10, 2, 12) four times, in both states: the numbers of Proposition 5.
Proof. (a) Polarity reverses inclusion, so A ⊂ P gives A* ⊃ P* = Q; every cell of A* is polar to a corner of A, a corner of P, so it lies in a cell wall of Q; and the five orbits are all of P's corners, so the five duals cut out exactly Q. (c) The plate of the dual face polar to an edge ab of a copy is the hyperplane perpendicular to a − b, and every edge of an S5-orbit shape runs along a root of that S5, whose mirror is a mirror of Q. (b), (e), (f) and the coverage of one pentagon and one edge in (d) are exact computations in the study, carried to all pentagons and edges by symmetry. Controls: with r² halved or doubled, P* has no corner on Q, and the fins of A* and B* cover no pentagon. ∎

The limits of the dual cats are these. Coverage of the pentagons comes from the shape type, not from the golden form: the fin over the dual face of an edge ab is the cone of directions in which a and b are both farthest, and every S5-orbit of the same type has the same cones, so the non-golden orbits (φ², φ², φ², 3/2, 0) and (φ², φ², 6/5, 6/5, 0) cover the pentagons too. What only the golden form gives is the exact corner fit: the duals' cells on Q's cell walls, A*'s corners on Q's corners, and Q as the intersection of the five duals; the non-golden orbits give none of these.

6. The chambers

Theorem C (the chambers). Exact counts, by Zaslavsky's sum of |Möbius| values [12] in exact Q(√5) arithmetic, confirmed by floating-point splitting and by a second floating-point count inserting the walls one at a time. The lattice data behind the counts are in §11.
(a) The 360 walls of the dual pair cut each Queen into 4,291,200 = 14400 × 298 chambers: 25 cross walls pass through one Coxeter chamber of the mirrors and cut it into 298 pieces of 15 combinatorial types (no two congruent, floating point). Each chamber is a pyramid from the centre over a piece of a cell.
(b) The 1560 walls of Theorem B cut Q into 593,236,800 = 14400 × 41,197 chambers; 174 walls pass through one Coxeter chamber.
(c) The characteristic polynomial of the 60 mirrors is (t − 1)(t − 11)(t − 19)(t − 29), the classical factorisation by the exponents of H4 [20], giving 14400 chambers; that of the 360 walls is (t − 1)(t³ − 359t² + 45959t − 2099281), whose cubic factor is irreducible over Q (2099281 = 29 · 191 · 379, and no divisor is a root).
(d) A's own 30 plates cut A into 3360 chambers, of which 120 are exactly the 600-cell's Coxeter chamber (in A's 5 tetrahedra, each cut by its 6 symmetry planes into 24); B's own 80 plates cut B into 62,880 chambers, none a Coxeter chamber of the 600-cell.

Walls through the centre cannot cut out a solid round the centre, so the solids A and B are never chambers; they are unions of chambers, A of 3360. That is the exact form of the third answer to the cat and the meat: by corners the five orbits are the 600-cell, by walls A and B carry all its mirrors, and by solids they are made of its chambers without being any one of them.

In every shape of Table B.2 that is not centrally symmetric, every plate with two or more faces is a wall of the host (the runcitruncated 5-cells' 5 shared sheets are cross walls; their 40 single fins are on no wall). Of the 15 uniform polytopes of H4 (Wythoff's construction; the complete list by Conway and Guy [5], proved complete by Möller [13]) all are heart and only the two Queens are hidden: the rectified, truncated and bitruncated members have 60 mirror plates and 1200 on no wall, the cantellated and cantitruncated 60 mirrors and 3720 or 4200 on no wall, the runcinated, runcitruncated and omnitruncated no plate on any wall; their mirror faces follow #107's Mirror Law [16].

Proposition 6 (Valley Charge, the golden station; the name is DICTO's). Exact. On DICTO's Hopscotch line S(μ) = 600-cell ∩ μ·120-cell between the Queens, the truncated-600-cell member at μ = 1/2 + √5/5, which cuts each edge at 1/2 − √5/10 = 1/(1 + φ²) of its length from each end, has 1440 corners, 720 cells, 2400 triangles and 1200 golden hexagons, and its Vacuus is exactly W(P): 20 hexagons on each mirror, 8 triangles on each cross wall.
Proof. Each hexagon lies inside a triangle of the 600-cell, so it is on a mirror (Lemma 3 a); the content is the cross-wall half, checked exactly: the corner triangles lie on the 300 cross walls, 8 on each. At truncation 1/3 or 1/5 instead, the 1200 triangle plates lie on no wall (floating point, review). ∎ Valley Charge was not found in the sources checked.

8. What "hidden" does and does not show

Hidden is common on the corners. By Lemma 3, a shape on the 600-cell's corners whose faces are edge triangles, golden rectangles, radius triangles or faces fixed by a reflection is hidden, whatever its harmonics. So hidden does not single out the silent shapes. Every full-dimensional orbit hull of Koca et al.'s five maximal subgroups of W(H4) [7] on the 600-cell's corners is hidden, except the 12- and 20-corner duopyramids (exact wall tests on floating-point hulls); the snub 24-cell, the grand antiprism and the special cuts are hidden and are not silent shapes.

The chance baseline. Random subsets of the corners: in 100 samples each, the mean share of plates on no wall is 28% for 20 corners and 20% for 30 corners, and no sample was all-wall; so all-wall is rare among random subsets of these sizes (under about 3% at this sample size), not impossible. For 60 corners the share is 2% and 60 of 100 samples were all-wall.

The ordinary orbits. The S5-orbits of (φ², φ², φ², 3/2, 0) and (φ², φ², 6/5, 6/5, 0) have 20 of 30 and 70 of 80 plates on no wall; but they are not on the 600-cell's corners, so they compare corners with non-corners, not twist with no twist.

Other shapes. R of Silent Running is C3; the four 30-point silence sets of the 600-cell are hidden; the 60-point ones are hidden, as most 60-point subsets are; the two 240-point silence sets of the 120-cell are not (240 and 420 plates on no wall).

9. Conjectures and open questions

  1. Conjecture (i). In a shape on a Queen's corners that is not centrally symmetric about the host centre, every shared sheet is a wall of the host. No shared sheet off the host's walls has been found; this is why one name, inherited heart, is kept.
  2. Conjecture (ii). There is one choice of ten S5s, one in each inscribed 600-cell, matched on all 25 shared 24-cells (Proposition 5 gives 1 in 60 for each pair).
  3. Conjecture (iii). The 1200 walls outside W(Q) are cross walls of the 120-cell's 225 inscribed 24-cells.
  4. Conjecture (iv). Valley Charge is the only two-ring seed of H4 whose Vacuus misses no wall.
  5. Questions. Is the mean-value condition of §3 sufficient for a shared sheet in every seed pattern? Which subsystems of the cross walls are free arrangements? How many selections of 60 A copies cover each triangle of a 600-cell exactly once? What are the classes and types of the 41,197 pieces, as for the 298?
  6. Prior art. Found earlier: the shapes, credited at each use; the W(A4) split of the 600-cell [7] and the orbits' Dynkin labels [8, §4.1, eq. (30)], which imply A's and B's Wythoff types but do not name or describe the hulls; the compound of ten 600-cells [10, 9]; the 25 inscribed 24-cells [9]; the 60 and 300 directions [18, 19]; the special cuts [6]; the Queens' faces on the mirrors and the Mirror Law (#107 [16]); C3's off-mirror plates (#108 [17]). Not found in the sources read (Sources checked, below): the Vacuus reading of any of these shapes, the index law, face lemmas (b) and (c) as statements about walls, the 24-cell reading of the cross walls, the copies in the 120-cell and their three classes of wall, the triangle split, the half-cat patterns, the chamber counts of the 360 and 1560 walls, Valley Charge, and the dual cats (their walls, kinds, covers and the A* corner partition).

10. Observations

Shadows. DICTO observed that "all of the shadows seem to carry interconnected lattices of possible vacuus". The section of #110 on this, with the wall counts of the four standard views, the connected graph of shadows (exact) and the paper's two shadow figures, is given in full in Chapter 5, §10 (Figures 5.4 and 5.5). The truncated icosahedron there is Archimedes' solid, named by Kepler [11].

Skeletons. DICTO proposed that Vacuus fins may form the Queens' skeletons. They do, for both Queens, each with her own cats. In the 600-cell every triangle is a face of a B copy (Theorem B (d)). In the 120-cell the fins of the dual copies cover every pentagon and every edge whole, on its own mirrors (Theorem B′ (d)). The inclusion copies of Theorem B, carried outward to the 120-cell's surface, cover its edges but none of its pentagons, and dualised they still cover none (Theorem B′ (e)): that is the false premise of §5, not a gap in the Red Queen's skeleton.

Volumes and overlaps. Exact. Of the 600-cell's volume, the hull of A is (1 + 3φ)/40, of B (3 + 5φ)/24 and of C3 (14 + 7φ)/60. The five hulls together cover 0.6504 of it (inclusion and exclusion over 31 exact intersections; Monte Carlo in the review 0.6511 ± 0.0005); their common part is a uniform bitruncated 5-cell, (23/750 + 23φ/1500) of it, and A ∩ B is a golden cantitruncated 5-cell, 0.1308 of it.

Censuses. In DICTO's White Queen atlas (#63, 62 slice types) and Red Queen atlas (124), with plates lifted along the slicing axis, most slices are partly hidden; the fully hidden ones are the 600-cell's corner-first slices with 32 and 12 corners and its cell-first tip tetrahedron, and the 120-cell's corner-first slices with 60 and 20 corners and two cell-first tetrahedra.

11. The intersection lattices behind Theorem C

Zaslavsky's theorem [12]: an arrangement of central hyperplanes in ℝ⁴ cuts space into |χ(−1)| chambers, where χ(t) = ΣX μ(X) tdim X runs over the flats X (intersections of walls), and μ is the Möbius function of the intersection lattice (μ = 1 on ℝ⁴, −1 on each wall, h − 1 on a plane in h walls, and on a line minus the sum over the flats above it). For the 360 walls of the dual pair (exact, in Q(√5); a second implementation, studies/2026-11-17-independent-checks/110-chambers, with and without symmetry reduction):

Table B.3. Flats of the 360 walls by rank. Planes and lines grouped by the number h of walls through them.
rankflatsby h (h: number)Σμ
01ℝ⁴1
136060 mirrors, 300 cross walls−360
235,0422: 27,000 · 3: 6,600 · 4: 450 · 5: 792 · 9: 200 (16 orbits; μ = h − 1)46,318
3803,1603: 478,800 · 4: 162,000 · 5: 72,000 · 6: 48,600 · 7: 18,000 · 8: 7,200 · 10: 9,840 · 12: 4,200 · 15: 1,200 · 16: 960 · 33: 300 · 45: 60 (149 orbits)−2,145,240
41the centre2,099,281

So χ(t) = t⁴ − 360t³ + 46318t² − 2145240t + 2099281 = (t − 1)(t³ − 359t² + 45959t − 2099281), and |χ(−1)| = 1 + 360 + 46318 + 2145240 + 2099281 = 4,291,200. The Σμ of each line class (h: Σμ) is −478,800, −352,800, −295,200, −248,400, −151,200, −97,200, −182,400, −115,200, −57,600, −60,240, −76,500 and −29,700, in the order of Table B.3; the per-orbit values are in the supporting data. A second count, inside one open Coxeter chamber of the mirrors, uses only the 25 cross walls that meet it: their restricted arrangement has Zaslavsky terms 1 + 25 + 138 + 134 = 298, and 14400 × 298 = 4,291,200.

For the 1560 walls of Theorem B the same two methods give χ(t) = (t − 1)(t³ − 1559t² + 1024679t − 295592161), |χ(−1)| = 593,236,800, and inside one Coxeter chamber the 174 walls that meet it have terms 1 + 174 + 5981 + 35041 = 41,197 (exact). The full list of line orbits (about 172 million lines) is not tabulated; the polynomial and the in-chamber terms are in the supporting data.

Chapter 2. Aristotle's Pyramid (#44, DICTO DOI 10.5281/zenodo.23529497)

Data and reproducibility

In studies/2026-10-21-aristotle-pyramids: pyramid_checks.py (exact arithmetic in ℚ(√5) with Python fractions; angles handled through cos² and the sign of cos; log pyramid-checks.log, 47 PASS, no FAIL): the solids' dihedral angles; every base and side angle of the five pyramids; the facts used in Lemma 2; the test over all 31 sets; the octet cell and its 8 + 8 pyramids; the slabs of the octet patch; the golden rhombohedron, Cleo-Icos, Antonius and their angles. angle_relations.py (angle-relations.log): the angles to 80 digits and a PSLQ search finding no integer relation among π, α, θD, θI. Appendix. The reference library searched

The library's manifests list 269 entries, of which 94 have a local file; these give the 81 distinct sources with a text layer listed below and 13 page images. The full text of the 81 sources was searched on 21 October 2026 for: Aristotle, De Caelo, On the Heavens, pyramid, fill space, space-filling, tile space, Dehn, dihedral angle, central pyramid, face pyramid, Maurolico, octet, tetrahedral-octahedral, golden rhombohedron, Cleo and Antonius. The 13 page images without a text layer (the patents of Fuller, Snelson, Pearce, Baer, Hogan, Bergman and Lalvani, and Fedorov 1885) and the 175 link-only entries were not searched.

About the author

DICTO is an artist in Osaka, Japan. His background is architectural sculpture, stonecarving and Buddhist statuary. He trained as an architectural stonecarver at the City and Guilds of London Art School (1998–2001), worked at St James's Palace, and recarved the Roman lettering of the Civil Service Rifles War Memorial at Somerset House, London, for its rededication in 2002. Since 2009 he has worked in Japan in the sculpture and restoration of Buddhist statues, among them a statue at Takaosan Jingo-ji, Kyoto, traditionally attributed to Kōbō Daishi Kūkai.

He builds geometric sculpture from recycled PET bottles with two modular systems of his own, B1NR and DICTOspheres, joined without glue. He holds six Japanese design registrations; five rest on spherical projections of the Platonic solids, the five forms behind his name: Dodecahedron, Icosahedron, Cube, Tetrahedron, Octahedron. His sculptures have been shown in Kyoto at the Kyoto Municipal Museum of Art (Interdependent Origination III, 2025), the Insho Domoto Museum of Fine Arts, the Kyoto Botanical Garden, the Kansho Exhibition and the Kyoto Takasegawa Art Festival.

The geometry behind the sculptures led to over a hundred papers, gathered in DICTO: Collected Geometric Works, and to the books of The Five Orders of Space, listed on the series page that closes this book. Every paper and book is published openly with its own DICTO DOI at Zenodo. More at https://dictospheres.com.

The Five Orders of Space

One work by DICTO in eleven books and twelve volumes, in five rings

The work is built after two models, credited as models. From Miyamoto Musashi’s Go Rin no Sho, The Book of Five Rings (五輪書, about 1645), it takes its five rings, Earth, Water, Fire, Wind and the Void. In paraphrase, not quoted from any translation: for Musashi, Earth lays out the way, Water is form and technique, Fire is the encounter, Wind is the knowledge of other schools, and the Void is what lies beyond. From Euclid’s Elements (about 300 BC) it takes its build: the Definitions are the DICTOnary (Book One, II: The DICTOnary of Atoms), the Postulates are Quanta of Space I (Book One, I: the manifesto), and the Propositions, with their proofs, are Books Two to Eleven. Each ring is one of the five Platonic solids, in the colour the Kaleidohedra app gives that solid.

Book Eleven. A Brief Geometry of Space

Opening

About 1645 the swordsman Miyamoto Musashi set down his way in five books: Earth, Water, Fire, Wind and the Void. Nineteen centuries before him Euclid had set down geometry as definitions, postulates and propositions, and ended by building the five regular solids and proving that there are no more. This work borrows from both. Its five rings are the five solids of DICTO’s name, each in the Kaleidohedra app’s colour for that solid: the cube for Earth, the icosahedron for Water, the tetrahedron for Fire, the octahedron for Wind, the dodecahedron for the Void. Earth lays out the pieces of space. Water follows them as they flow into one another. Fire is the meeting: two neighbours change places, and a cell of living skin is carried into four dimensions. Wind goes back to the old schools and their five solids. The Void goes beyond them, and comes home. One question runs through all five: what stays in place when the parts of space change places?

For a newcomer

One book from each ring, then the rest, with the DICTOnary at hand throughout:

  1. Book One, Quanta of Space I, the essay (Earth)
  2. Book Two, Space-Fillers and Lattice Moves (Earth)
  3. Book Six, HexOct: The Hexagonality Law (Fire)
  4. Book Seven, The Permutohedron Paradigm (Fire)
  5. Book Eight, The Platonic Solids (Wind)
  6. Book Eleven, A Brief Geometry of Space (Void)

A reader who knows the five solids may start at Book Eight. The Water books and Books Nine and Ten are taken when the main road points to them. The full order is the numbering.

Models: Miyamoto Musashi, Go Rin no Sho (The Book of Five Rings, 五輪書, about 1645); Euclid, Elements (about 300 BC), Book XIII for the five solids. Colours as in DICTO’s square, the Kaleidohedra app’s colours of the five solids.
